#233. Apple may be developing its own AI agent that runs your Mac

Apple appears to be working on its own AI agent to conduct computer work on behalf of users — including using apps, accessing websites, and coding, among other things.

The news was originally reported by Bloomberg, which spotted a job listing the iPhone maker recently posted for a senior engineering manager to oversee the creation of an “AI agent that can use a computer.”

“Imagine an AI agent that can use a computer the way a capable person does,” the posting says. “It browses the web, works across apps, runs commands, writes and debugs code, and calls services, and it chooses the right tool for each step. It does all this privately and with the polish people expect from Apple.”

The posting additionally notes that the agents should be able to move “fluidly between tools” and “carry out multi-step tasks across apps, browsers, command lines, code, and APIs.”

TechCrunch reached out to Apple for more information but did not receive a response.

Agents capable of computer work have become one of the hottest commodities in the tech industry over the past year. Major labs like Anthropic and OpenAI have launched such services, while other major tech brands — like Meta, which recently launched its Muse agent line — have also moved into the space. It makes sense that Apple — which is trying to expand its AI offerings in the new John Ternus era — would want to follow suit.

Bloomberg additionally notes that, at the same time that Apple is working on its own digital assistant, the company is making it significantly harder for third-party agents to gain total control over its computers.

The outlet notes that the version of its upcoming macOS 27.3 will deploy warnings discouraging users from granting full access to third-party agents. In October, Apple issued a notification to developers stating that the company planned to “introduce additional controls to ensure that users who genuinely wish to grant an app this extraordinary level of access can only do so with very explicit user action.”

Bloomberg writes that while the iPhone maker “has eyed introducing the service as soon as the end of next year,” progress on its in-house agent “remains in the early stages.”

#232. Amazon’s first digital photo frame is up to 50 percent off for Black Friday

The nifty frame lets you pull up specific photos using only your voice or attach voice recordings to individual shots.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Amazon only announced its first digital photo frame less than a month ago, but it’s already getting a sizable discount for Black Friday. You can currently grab the Alexa Photo Frame at Amazon and Best Buy starting at $114 ($115 off), making it a great holiday gift for anyone who loves displaying family photos. If you prefer the walnut trim over the birch or standard black models, it’s also on sale at Best Buy for $134.99 ($95 off) — which, like with the others, is the first discount we’ve seen.

The Alexa Photo Frame features a 10-inch, semi-matte touchscreen that’s designed to make digital photos look more natural, with built-in light sensors that automatically adjust brightness throughout the day. It displays images from Amazon Photos, which supports uploads from both iOS and Android devices, including photos stored in iCloud. You can choose which people or albums to display, or let Alexa Plus do the work, which often results in photos based on your facial expression, the season, and even the lighting. You can also search for specific photos with voice commands, letting you easily pull up images of your first dog, your child as a baby, and other shots without having to scroll endlessly through years of photos yourself.

If you want to add a more personal touch, Amazon also lets you attach voice notes to individual photos via the Amazon Photos app. For example, you can add a recording of your kid explaining their Halloween costume to a trick-or-treating photo. Plus, you can preload the frame with photos and recordings before gifting it, so your loved one won’t have to spend time setting it up.

We’re still testing Amazon’s answer to the Aura frame, but it looks like a particularly good option if you’re already an Amazon Prime member. A Prime subscription grants you unlimited full-resolution photo storage and access to Alexa Plus at no additional cost, and unlike Amazon’s smart displays, the frame doesn’t serve up annoying ads. That said, you don’t need a subscription to use it, and new users can always access a limited-time trial of Alexa Plus.

#231. Three El Salvador soldiers die in explosives accident in Haiti

The soldiers were part of a Salvadoran military contingent deployed to the violence-ridden Caribbean nation last month to rescue four missing airmen. The deployment has widened into an operation against Haiti's gangs.

Three Salvadoran soldiers were killed in Haiti while handling explosives, El Salvador's military said on Friday.

The incident marks the first officially reported deaths since a 2,000-member Salvadoran military contingent was deployed to the gang violence-stricken Caribbean nation last month.

One of the soldiers succumbed to his injuries while being treated in the neighboring Dominican Republic, where he was airlifted to, the El Salvador military said on X.

Another soldier was wounded in the incident and is out of danger, it added.

It was not immediately clear where the blast took place.

The Salvadoran military's statement also did not give any further details on the type of explosives the soldiers were working with.

"We extend our deepest condolences and solidarity to the families of the three deceased," it said.

Salvadoran President Nayib Bukele sent over 2,000 troops and special forces to Haiti to rescue four El Salvador airmen — members of the UN-backed Gang Suppression Force (GSF)—  who went missing after a gang ambush on October 18 in the Haitian capital of Port-au-Prince.

The additional troops dispatched by Bukele are not part of the GSF, and the contingent's remit appears to have broadened to stamp out Haitian gangs.

Bukele, who has been known for his tough approach to the gangs in El Salvador, has posted several videos of strikes on residences of gang leaders around Port-au-Prince.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Meanwhile, on Friday, Haiti announced an agreement with El Salvador to build three new Haitian prisons.

The development follows this week's visit to El Salvador by Haiti's security and justice minister, Patrick Pelissier.

The prisons will be built by a Salvadoran company in collaboration with a Haitian firm, Pelissier said.

Haitian prisons are notoriously overcrowded. In 2024, gang raids in two of the country's biggest penitentiaries saw thousands of inmates being released.

El Salvador, on the other hand, is home to a notorious maximum security mega-prison known as Terrorism Confinement Center (CECOT).

The country has imprisoned over 90,000 people with no access to family visits ever since entering a state of exception in 2022.

Pelissier visited CECOT during his trip to El Salvador.

"Once you enter, there is no hope of ever seeing the outside world again," the Haitian minister said as he praised the mega-prison.

#230. Uncovering the Hidden Origins of the Placenta

Where Readers Share Great Links. Join Free

Caltech biologists used mouse embryos to map implantation in detail and found that a small group of cells from the embryo’s opposite side also helps form the placenta, overturning a long-held model. The work may help explain very early pregnancy loss and reveal limits of current embryo-like stem cell models.

#229. The US national cyber director is stepping down

White House national cyber director Sean Cairncross will be stepping down from his role, Axios reported. The news comes as Democrats prepare to take control of Congress and as businesses across the country report a flurry of technical outages whose cause remains unknown.

Cairncross, the president’s principal adviser on national cybersecurity policy and strategy, took office in August 2025. Since then, he has met with top AI leaders and helped the Trump administration make key AI decisions, according to Axios. The outlet also reports that the split between Cairncross and the White House was amicable, and his departure was timed so that the White House could nab a Senate confirmation for his role before Democrats take control of Congress in January.

Cairncross, a lawyer and longtime Republican operative, had no cybersecurity background when the Senate confirmed him 59–35. He previously served as the Republican National Committee’s chief operating officer and, during Trump’s first term, as CEO of the Millennium Challenge Corporation, a federal foreign aid agency. The office hasn’t been around long; Congress created it in early 2021, and Cairncross was only its third Senate-confirmed director. The first director, Chris Inglis, left after about 19 months, and the job then went about 10 months without a permanent director.

We’ve reached out to the White House for comment.

#228. California AG subpoenas Nvidia over worries about Huang’s ties with China

California’s Attorney General Rob Bonta on Thursday evening said the state has served an investigative subpoena to Nvidia following news of former CEO Jensen Huang’s affair with a woman who the U.S. government believes is a Chinese spy.

The state is investigating the company to find out if its advanced GPUs or confidential information may have been shared with China, and if the company had somehow misled its investors and the public.

“The conduct of Nvidia and its former CEO raises bright red flags, including about the ways in which the company may have misled investors,” Bonta said in a statement.

“My office is investigating whether Nvidia or Jensen Huang’s actions resulted in China obtaining advanced chips or confidential information — and if Nvidia misled investors and the public about this risk, in violation of the law,” he added.

The investigation comes in the wake of a messy couple of weeks for Nvidia, which has overnight come under intense scrutiny around the world due to the implications of Huang’s alleged relationship with the Chinese national, Alice Huang (who is not related to Jensen).

The Nvidia founder resigned last week after further revelations of extramarital affairs with the company’s employees. And while many tech voices have been petitioning Nvidia to bring back Huang as CEO, saying they see him as indispensable to the further development of the AI industry, the company’s new chair, Harvey Jones, said earlier this week that Nvidia had no intention of reinstating Huang to his position.

The company’s board has hired a law firm as well as an independent slate of directors to investigate the extent of the damage, and the U.S. government is trying to find out the extent of Alice Huang’s ties with the Chinese government.

The U.S. Congress is also gearing up to subpoena Huang, seeking questions about the affair, and the SEC is said to be launching a civil probe into the company, trying to find out if Nvidia’s public disclosures could have misled investors.

Democratic representatives have also opened an inquiry into the Trump administration’s management of Nvidia’s chip sales to China. In a scathing letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, Democrats last week asked the Commerce Department to preserve records about an arrangement that they called questionable and illegal.

“Nvidia is in a messy, transnational situation but the underlying principle of our corporate securities law is clear: Companies cannot mislead their investors,” Bonta wrote.

Huang has agreed to testify before Congress after several senior lawmakers threatened to subpoena him.

You probably know John Roberts best as the voice of Linda Belcher on Bob’s Burgers. The show just wrapped up its 16th season and shows no signs of slowing down anytime soon. The show remains a reliable source of laughs, top-notch puns, and frankly wholesome fun.

Comedy is just part of Roberts’ life, however. In addition to his appearances on Gravity Falls and Comedy Bang! Bang!, he’s also a musician with deep roots in New York City’s queer nightlife scene. He just released his latest record, I Am, on Friday, and it dabbles in ’80s pop, ’90s house, and even dashes of ’00s rave. But while the record sounds made for the dance floor, it actually tackles some pretty heavy subject matter, like mortality, aging, and grief following the loss of his mother.

Despite that, Roberts is still having fun, as you can see in the video for “Complicated,” which revels in the queer cult camp of Scorpio Rising, William Friedkin’s Cruising, and The Wolf Man.

#224. Behind Jensen Huang’s exit, questions about a management style reportedly built on fear

Earlier this week at the DealBook conference in New York, Elon Musk, beamed in from a trip to China with President Donald Trump, was asked about the ouster of his longtime friend, former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang. Musk compared it to kicking Michael Jordan off the Chicago Bulls’ roster during his historic run. Of Nvidia’s employees, he said, “They love [Huang]. They love him.”

Well, maybe not all of them. Thousands of employees have signed a letter asking for Huang’s return, but others tell The New York Times he ruled through praise and fear, as well as by humiliating people.

According to the Times, Huang told subordinates that Nvidia’s wealth meant it wasn’t possible to motivate employees with money, so humiliation was the only lever left. He reportedly told one executive his mother should have aborted him and told another employee he would rip his arms off and beat him to death with them.

Naturally, over a 33-year career, regrettable things may have been said, but the Times found a long paper trail that suggests the incidents weren’t isolated. In 2005, for example, Michael Winston, a former HR chief, threatened to sue and called Huang’s conduct the most abusive he’d seen. Nvidia paid him more than $1 million. Huang’s spokesman calls the allegations fabrications.

#222. Man convicted of arson over deadly 2025 Palisades fire in Los Angeles

A 30-year-old man has been convicted of causing one of the most destructive wildfires in the history of Los Angeles. The verdict came after the case was retried.

A US-French man has been convicted on federal arson charges connected with the 2025 Palisades fire in Los Angeles, California, which killed 12 people and devastated the seaside enclave of Pacific Palisades.

The weeks-long blaze destroyed some 6,800 homes and other structures, causing estimated property losses of $150 billion (€136.7 billion).

A US District Court jury in Los Angeles found the man guilty of starting a hilltop brush fire on January 1, 2025.

While the fire was initially suppressed, it continued smoldering underground and reignited days later amid fierce winds to become the highly destructive Palisades Fire.

The man is set to be sentenced on March 17. First Assistant US Attorney Bill Essayli told reporters that he could face up to 40 years in prison.

The man was arrested in October 2025, and already faced one trial five months ago that ended with a hung jury. The judge declared a mistrial in June, prompting federal prosecutors to retry the case under modified charges.

Prosecutors said security camera footage and location data from the defendant's cellphone showed he was the only person nearby when ​the January 1 blaze began in the Santa Monica Mountains National Recreation Area.

At both trials, they characterized the man, a former Uber driver, as a loner who held a deep resentment against society, and particularly the wealthy.

The defense argued that the initial blaze could have been caused by fireworks, while prosecutors said there was no evidence that any had been set off in the area.

In March, the man denied in a court declaration that he had willfully started any fire, though he declined to testify in ⁠his own defense at either trial.

The Palisades blaze erupted almost at the same time as another wildfire that broke out in Eaton Canyon, some 30 miles (48 km) to the east.

That fire, believed to have been ignited by sparks from an electrical transmission tower, killed 19 people and devastated the Los Angeles suburb of Altadena.

Together, the two blazes rank as the deadliest wildfire disaster ever seen in Los Angeles County.

#221. The FBI and USDA were reportedly hacked

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The list of hacks hitting the US government over the last few months keeps growing.

The list of hacks hitting the US government over the last few months keeps growing.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Friday, it was reported that both the US Department of Agriculture (USDA) and the FBI were hacked. In reporting on the FBI hack, CNN noted that this is the second such attack on the agency in the last few months, this time targeting the National Crime Information Center (NCIC), which federal, state, and local law enforcement use to share information, including warrants and missing persons. President Donald Trump later confirmed the hack in an interview with Fox News. NBC first reported the USDA hack on Friday. According to Reuters:

While the payments ​database is the known extent ​of the intrusion so far, the former ‌officials ⁠warned that the compromised system could provide a backdoor to more sensitive information, including food safety ​data and ​research on ⁠animal-borne illnesses, according to the report.

The breaches come following a week of major outages across several sectors, including at the FAA and NSA, which the government has sought to reassure the public were not the result of a cyberattack.

President Trump suggested that the breach of the FBI might not be an entirely bad thing, telling Fox News that, “Maybe we’ll learn about the voter fraud, all the voter fraud that took place,” and “wouldn’t it be great to learn about Russia, Russia, Russia?” referring to investigations into his ties to Russia.

The FBI suffered a breach, reported in late September, that allegedly compromised personal records of “all FBI employees and applicants.” This was followed by a hack of DHS, reported in October, comprising 40,000 employee records. In the wake of these major outages, the White House national cyber director, Sean Cairncross, stepped down this week.

#220. Six AI-powered gadgets that could make your home office smarter

Tech companies are racing to figure out how AI can make everyday work a little easier.

From notetakers to webcams that follow your movements, here are six AI-powered gadgets worth checking out if you want to upgrade your workspace.

If you’re constantly scribbling notes during meetings, the Flowtica Scribe could save you from having to type everything up afterward.

The AI-powered pen combines traditional handwriting with audio recording. You can use its built-in gel pen to take notes, tap the side to highlight important points, and press the end of the pen to start recording audio. Once the meeting is over, its AI can transcribe the conversation, summarize key takeaways, and create a to-do list based on what was discussed.

You can even ask the AI questions about the meeting to find specific information without having to go through the entire recording.

The Scribe comes with a built-in microphone and 32GB of onboard storage. It can also identify up to 15 different speakers and supports 92 languages.

Anker’s AI-powered recording pin, Soundcore Work, is designed to capture conversations, meetings, and quick voice memos without taking up much space on your desk.

Priced at $159 (currently reduced to $99 in the Amazon Black Friday sale), the coin-sized device comes with a puck-shaped charging case and can record for up to eight hours on a single charge, extending to 32 hours when used with its case. Anker also claims a recording range of up to 5 meters, making it useful for capturing conversations around a meeting table.

The companion app handles transcription and uses AI to turn recordings into more organized notes and summaries. Users also get 300 minutes of free audio transcription each month.

If you need more recording capacity or access to additional AI features, Anker offers optional subscription plans, including Pro ($99 per year) and Unlimited ($239 per year).

Logitech’s MX Keypad brings AI shortcuts directly to your desk, with dedicated buttons that let you launch your preferred AI assistant or trigger specific productivity tools with a single press. If you frequently switch between different apps and AI assistants, having these shortcuts within reach could make your workday easier.

The keypad can be configured to work with tools such as GitHub Copilot and Claude Code, along with a range of popular productivity and creative apps, such as OpenAI Codex, Microsoft Teams, PowerPoint, Excel, Notion, Slack, Photoshop, Final Cut Pro, Zoom, and Figma.

Amazon’s Kindle Scribe Colorsoft brings color to the company’s digital notetaking lineup and offers some neat AI features designed to clean up your messy notes.

Starting at $479, the device is aimed at people who regularly work with digital documents, including students, researchers, and professionals who prefer annotating files rather than printing them out. It lets you highlight important passages, mark up documents, organize handwritten notes, and sketch out ideas.

Notably, the Scribe Colorsoft incorporates AI-powered tools to clean up messy handwriting, straighten highlighting and underlining, summarize notes, and help turn scattered thoughts into more organized information. Its search feature can look across your saved notes to find specific details and help identify connections between different entries.

If you’re tired of adjusting your webcam during video calls, Insta360’s Link 2 Pro aims to take care of that for you.

Priced at $249 (discounted to $199 for Black Friday), this AI-powered webcam combines 4K video capture with intelligent tracking and automatic framing. Its smart video tracker can follow your movements and adjust the camera’s position to keep you in view, even when you move around your workspace, which is particularly useful for people who regularly give presentations, teach online, or switch between sitting at their desk and standing during video calls.

The camera also offers group tracking, which can come in handy during team meetings or presentations involving multiple people. AI-powered noise cancellation is another useful addition, helping reduce distracting background sounds during calls.

Your desk could probably use a little intelligence, too. Autonomous Desk Pro is an adjustable standing desk designed to encourage healthier habits throughout the workday.

Beyond switching between sitting and standing positions, the AI-powered desk uses built-in sensors to monitor your activity and remind you when it’s time to move, stretch, or change positions. It can track how much time you spend sitting versus standing, giving you a better idea of your daily work habits.

The AI features go a step further by allowing you to ask questions about your activity. For example, you can check how long you’ve been sitting, compare your activity with the previous day, or get information about the room’s CO2 levels to help determine whether it’s time to open a window.

#219. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

#217. A transcription firm used by police, prosecutors, and tech giants exposed customer data online

A transcription company that caters to law enforcement, government departments, and Silicon Valley tech giants has resolved a security lapse after leaving a large amount of internal data, including customer transcriptions and their source materials, exposed to the web.

The data security lapse at San Francisco-based Tigerfish exposed transcriptions of police interviews, body-cam footage, and customers’ raw audio and video footage. The exposed data also included invoices of the company’s customers, which include police departments and prosecutors’ offices.

According to Anurag Sen, a good faith security researcher who found the data, the files were located on an Amazon-hosted (S3) cloud server that was openly listed and accessible to anyone via an easy-to-guess web address — no passwords needed.

The security lapse underscores how simple flaws or misconfigurations can result in sensitive information getting exposed online, especially as AI makes it easier to uncover weaknesses that can put companies and their data at risk.

Sen discovered the exposed data online in October and alerted the company to the issue, but did not hear back. Sen later provided details to TechCrunch in an effort to flag the incident with the company for resolution.

Sen, 30, is no stranger to TechCrunch. For the past decade, he has searched for sensitive datasets exposed online with the goal of notifying the companies and organizations that their information is open to the internet. Like many security researchers, much of his efforts are driven by a duty to secure the internet, and he describes his work as an “unpaid air-traffic controller.”

From his island home in Indonesia, Sen has uncovered sensitive U.S. military emails spilling from a compartmentalized Department of Defense cloud server; databases exposing reams of customer records; the inner workings of a country’s national license plate surveillance system; and public systems that inadvertently sent out streams of SMS codes used by people around the world to log into their online accounts.

Given Sen’s propensity for finding often highly confidential information online, we verified the source of the leak and the data, and contacted Tigerfish.

Soon after making contact, Tigerfish secured its exposed S3 cloud bucket.

“We’ve blocked all public access to the bucket you identified, including directory listings and individual files,” Tigerfish president, Adam Goldberg, said in an email. “We’ve also verified that all of our other S3 buckets are blocked to public access.”

“We’re continuing to review and strengthen our storage security more broadly,” added Goldberg.

The company said that Sen’s email disclosing the data leak was caught in its spam filter.

It’s unclear if the company has notified its customers. Tigerfish did not respond to a follow-up email.

Discoveries of exposed datasets have consumed a large part of Sen’s early cybersecurity career so far. He scours the web daily using automated scanners and specialist search engines to identify databases, cloud storage, or other sensitive information that likely shouldn’t be left unprotected online.

The modern web runs on absolute trust, he said, and operates under the notion that the world’s data is kept secure. But he described how his findings reveal the reality of how “fragile” the internet is behind the scenes.

Despite the recent focus on how AI is accelerating people’s ability to discover vulnerabilities, Sen said that many of the data leaks and exposures that he finds come down to basic human errors.

“An engineer spinning up a test cloud database on a Friday, disabling authentication to test an API, and forgetting to shut the door before heading into the weekend,” was just one example, he recounted.

Sen’s findings over the years highlight the ongoing problems caused by such security mistakes, just as the new wave of vibe-coding and AI-generated development is creating new security problems due to insecure and unchecked code deployed to the web.

Similar to Amazon’s recent efforts to make it difficult to make S3 buckets public without going through clear multistep warnings, newer vibe-coding platforms like Lovable and Supabase have also made changes to their platforms to prevent user mistakes.

“Everyone often fears shadowy hackers,” Sen said. But he argues that the real crisis is leaving sensitive systems and databases “wide open to the public internet without a password.”

#214. Bending Spoons buys Mailchimp for $3.68B

Bending Spoons has bought up another distressed internet company. This time, it’s the email marketing firm Mailchimp, bought for $3.68 billion from the tax-preparation giant Intuit.

Intuit acquired the company in 2021 for roughly $12 billion, so it’s taking quite a loss — and, perhaps, Bending Spoons is getting quite a bargain. At the time of the sale to Intuit, that acquisition caused headlines because Mailchimp employees reportedly received no equity, so no share of the sale proceeds, because the founders had allegedly promised them they didn’t need it as they would never sell.

Flash forward to now, with Intuit hit hard by the Death Star to SaaS trade — aka AI — with the company’s shares worth less than half what they were a year ago. The company told Reuters that the sale will let it sharpen its focus on AI tools for small businesses, and the new cash will be used to repurchase shares.

Bending Spoons describes the transaction as an all-cash deal, although that’s not quite true: As part of the same package, Intuit is buying $368 million in newly minted shares of Bending Spoons, so the net effect is a split of 90% cash to 10% stock.

Still, the transaction is a major pickup for Bending Spoons, which has become a kind of clearinghouse for late-stage web properties, with its game plan to hold them long term. In just the last year, the company has snapped up AOL, Eventbrite, Airtable, and Miro. Previous acquisitions include older stalwarts like Evernote, Meetup, and Vimeo. In each case, Bending Spoons integrated the products into centralized engineering and revenue systems, resulting in significant cost savings while often improving the available support and product resources.

“We want to place ourselves as an operator that takes beloved brands and makes them much better,” Bending Spoons co-founder and chief product officer, Matteo Danieli, told TechCrunch in July.

#212. Chips can survive the space environment — can their financing?

The push to build data centers in space faces plenty of obstacles, from tricky engineering to a dearth of rockets that could take chips to orbit. But what about paying for the chips?

The data center ecosystem currently depends on debt financing, with cloud providers borrowing to build out data centers and then paying back the loans with a share of the revenue.

Those deals typically use the chips as collateral — if the data centers default on their loans, the lenders can take possession of the chips and resell them. That backstop makes it cheaper for companies to finance their chips. Even older chips retain value — according to Compute Credit Index Research, some H100s are selling for about half of their original cost basis from 2023, while some H200 configurations are selling for the same price they did when introduced in 2024.

But you’re not going to repossess chips installed in a satellite orbiting 300 miles above Earth at 17,500 miles per hour. The Financial Times is reporting that lenders behind SpaceX’s $40 billion chip financing deal asked that none of the chips purchased be used for the company’s orbital data center plans. It’s not clear if they succeeded in getting SpaceX to agree with the provision.

That may not matter to the company in the near term — SpaceX’s ability to deploy compute at terrestrial ground stations has proven lucrative, and on the company’s most recent earnings call, CEO Elon Musk detailed how he was dispatching engineers from the company’s rocket team to accelerate that build-out by taking on challenges like casting turbine blades for generators at faster than existing suppliers.

However, open questions remain about how easy it will be to find land and power to support the data center build-out. Many potential projects face delays and local opposition, while markets wait to see the state of compute demand and the ability of its buyers to turn it into revenue.

Musk, meanwhile, began saying at the beginning of 2026 that he believes space will be the “cheapest” place to operate data centers in two to three years. That’s unlikely until SpaceX can fly its Starship rocket in a fully reusable configuration. In its most recent flight test, the company failed to recover the booster stage but did succeed in landing the orbital stage of the rocket back at its launch facility in Texas. It’s not clear, though, when the second stage will fly again or how expensive it will be to prepare it for a second trip to space.

Even with Starship, however, SpaceX — as well as startups like StarCloud, Cowboy Space Corporation, Sophia Space, Orbital, and others — will need to buy chips. If the already daunting economics of space data centers weren’t challenging enough, it looks like they’ll be spending more to buy them than their terrestrial counterparts.

That might be an advantage for Google, which is focused on deploying its own TPU chips in space through Project Suncatcher. SpaceX and Tesla are working together to build their own chip foundry, Terafab, in the U.S., but that project has yet to break ground and will require years and billions of dollars before it starts producing semiconductors at scale.

#211. Super-hot rock geothermal could end data center energy woes

Geothermal startups are in a race to see who can tap the deepest, hottest rocks. The winners could power the next phase of the grid, data centers and all.

The broader markets got their first taste of enhanced geothermal earlier this year when Fervo Energy went public. But this week was all about super-hot rock geothermal.

Geothermal has become a darling among investors, tech companies, and even the Trump administration. The basic technology has been around for decades, but advances in drilling techniques borrowed from the oil and gas industry has supercharged it. The U.S. has plenty of potential, too, with up to 57 terawatts of generating capacity, more than enough to power the next wave of AI data centers.

An emerging class of geothermal energy, known as super-hot rock, could help the industry unlock much of that potential.

The secret to super-hot rock geothermal is what happens to water at extreme depths. As it gets closer to the superheated core of the Earth, both temperature and pressure rise significantly. When they get high enough, ordinary water turns into a supercritical fluid — a sort of gaseous-liquid state that can carry tremendous amounts of energy. A single super-hot rock well could generate 15 megawatts of electricity or more.

Super-hot rock geothermal is inching closer to commercial reality. Quaise Energy said on Tuesday that it had landed Google as a customer. The hyperscaler said it would pay more than $500 million to secure 50 megawatts of power from a Quaise project in central Oregon, which will tap into searing heat three miles deep.

Google is paying a premium for the amount of power Quaise will deliver, but that’s in line with the company’s strategy of getting in early with potential suppliers. For instance, it was an investor in and an early customer of Fervo.

Meanwhile, on Monday, Khosla-backed Mazama Energy announced it had figured out how to keep its drill heads cool as they tap into super-hot rocks. And last week, XGS Energy was given a grant by the Department of Energy to develop and refine materials, well designs, and models to improve reliability at depths where super-hot rock exists.

XGS is dipping its toes in the super-hot waters, while Mazama and Quaise have made it their core focus, though they’re taking very different approaches.

Quaise’s technology sounds like science fiction: Starting at about 2.5 miles deep, the company will swap out its conventional drill bit for one powered by a gyrotron, bombarding rock with millimeter-wave radiation and turning it into ash. The hope is that the technology can make drilling under super-hot conditions cost-effective, something that’s challenging with traditional metal drill bits, which wear out faster at those temperatures.

Mazama, on the other hand, thinks that traditional drill bits will work just fine if they can be kept cool.

The startup’s newly announced technology, Carbon Chill, uses carbon dioxide in place of water-based drilling “mud” to keep the bit cool. At those depths and temperatures, the carbon dioxide turns supercritical, allowing it to hold significant amounts of energy. Just above the drill bit, the CO2 flows through a valve that looks similar to a rocket nozzle. As the gas flows through the valve and expands on the other end, the expansion rapidly cools it, allowing it to absorb significant amounts of heat. The CO2 also removes drill cuttings on its trip to the surface, where it’s filtered and reused.

In both cases, Quaise and Mazama are betting that their technology could leapfrog today’s state-of-the-art commercial geothermal power plants. By going deeper and hotter, they’re hoping to get substantially more power out of each well. Given that drilling accounts for about a quarter to a third of a geothermal power plant’s total costs, drilling fewer wells could make a project more competitive.

Investors have been betting that the technologies will soon prove themselves. Quaise has raised more than $270 million from investors, including Prelude Ventures, Collaborative Fund, and Engine Ventures, with Vinod Khosla stepping in as an angel investor, according to PitchBook. Mazama has raised $171 million from investors, including Khosla Ventures, Doerr Capital, and Bill Gates.

As with any new technology, there’s some risk that the new techniques won’t pay off. But early results suggest they will — Mazama drilled its second well 80% faster than its first, and its CO2-cooling tech cut drilling time another 20%, co-founder and CEO Sriram Vasantharajan told TechCrunch.

That, plus Google’s vote of confidence in Quaise, suggests the race to super-hot rock geothermal is, well, heating up.

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

#205. AI claimed a solution to the 167-year-old Riemann hypothesis — and a human did, too

It’s a big week for Levent Alpöge: The mathematician saw his employer, Anthropic, become the first publicly traded frontier AI lab. He also appears to have solved one of the most important problems in mathematics.

“The precise timing is horrible,” he wrote in an essay announcing his findings and describing how he messaged Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei last Sunday night to ask how to announce that he had found a Lean-compilable solution to the Riemann hypothesis, which dates back to 1859 and is among the Millennium Prize math problems.

The hypothesis conjectures that there is a hidden structure underlying the appearance of prime numbers. If the claimed result holds true, we’re entering sublime-mysteries-of-the-universe territory.

Maybe the best way to understand how exciting this is to the mathematics community is to watch the experts puzzling through the result in a chat on Zulip. Mathematicians like Chris Birkbeck, a number theorist at the University of East Anglia, are posting thoughts like this …

The cohomology bound is proved by what looks like an automorphy-lifting argument. It genuinely uses the existence of Galois representations attached to regular algebraic, polarized cuspidal automorphic representations of GL_n over CM fields, along with base change, Jacquet–Shalika, Clozel’s purity, Bernstein–Zelevinsky, the main theorem of CM, Deligne–Ribet and more.

For my part, I tapped out of mathematics at college calculus, so I can’t help decode exactly what that means.

But that comment was followed by 20 readers reacting 🤯.

It will be a while before the mathematics community feels like it has a handle on the proof, but most appear confident that the formalized Lean result will hold. Lean is an an open-source coding language used to express mathematical ideas as code, allowing proofs to be formally verified. Several participants in the Zulip chat, including MIT mathematician Andrew Sutherland, say they have successfully confirmed that Alpöge’s solution can be compiled successfully.

Alpöge appeared in his essay to feel a bit guilty about releasing the proof without a truly human-intelligible explanation, but his obsession with the problem since childhood made him feel he “could not live” if he waited to release the finding.

Alpöge described a “stream-of-consciousness jaunt through the experience of finding all the pieces of the argument through incredible confusion with Claude” with more than a hint of AI euphoria, describing how the model “flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line.”

However, for all that action to mean anything, we’ll have to wait for humans to be able to understand the result and explain it to each other, something Alpöge has committed to pursue.

Alpöge and a collaborator, NYU mathematician Tristan Buckmaster, also released another important proof related to the Navier-Stokes equations, problems that helped kick off the race between frontier labs and mathematicians to solve outstanding problems this summer.

Meanwhile, Ravi Vakil, a Stanford professor and the president of the American Mathematics Association, posted a proof of the General Riemann hypothesis written by Stanford postdoc Alessandro Masullo, noting that “it is all human, with no AI. The hope was to bring Alessandro to Stanford for a year to polish the writing and then announce it after running it by enough people. Now it seems best to put out the current draft.”

Without being expressed in Lean, it is too early to say whether the proof checks out, much less if anyone understands it.

In a brief introduction to his proof, which he said is the result of 12 years of work, Masullo wrote that “if any message at all this work is meant to send to the mathematician who feels discouraged by the pace and tone of the current climate, it is this: we are still here. We are very much alive. We are not going anywhere.”

This story has been updated to include that Alpöge’s Lean proof has been checked by independent sources.

#204. 'Killer robots' talks at UN end with no legally binding ban

Countries have agreed to extend discussions over controls on lethal autonomous weapons systems for two years.

United Nations talks on regulating lethal autonomous weapons systems ended on Saturday without an agreement on a legally binding ban.

The negotiations did, however, yield an agreement for further discussions to develop new rules by 2028.

Delegates from 128 states that are parties to the UN Convention on Certain Conventional Weapons (CCW) had been meeting in Geneva since Monday amid rising concerns over the use of the so-called killer robots in global conflicts.

The conference ended early on Saturday in what campaigners deemed a "weakened" outcome, following several hours of overnight parleying.

Nearly 100 nations had called for negotiations on a legally binding agreement on the systems capable of killing without a human making the final decision to fire.

UN Secretary-General António Guterres, Pope Leo XIV and the Red Cross had also supported a ban on killer robots.

However, countries that have the technology, including arms-producing giants like the ​US, Russia ‌and Israel, were among those opposing the mandate.

Ultimately, the 128 countries  "agreed to launch the elaboration of an international instrument on lethal autonomous weapons systems, which should be conducted over the next two years", the conference's Swiss chair, Julien Thoni, told the media on Saturday.

But while Thoni hailed a multilateral diplomacy delivering "concrete results" even in "today's deeply divided world," rights groups expressed disappointment at the minimal agreement.

"States adopted a watered-down mandate that fails to chart a clear path towards a legally binding instrument," news agency AFP quoted Stop Killer Robots, a global coalition of over 300 organizations, as saying.

"Over 95 states expressed their support for a treaty. In the final hours, a small number of states weakened the mandate introducing uncertainty about the legal status of the final document," it added.

The group had earlier said that autonomous weapons systems were already reaching the battlefield and such systems had been tested or deployed, including in the Ukraine war.

#203. Daniel Ek backs AI researcher’s fusion power startup. Here’s what they’re building.

Spotify founder Daniel Ek is backing Europa Fusion, a stealthy fusion power startup founded by an AI researcher who is taking a different approach to developing a fusion power plant.

The U.K.-based startup was founded in February, though sources told TechCrunch that the founder, Nantas Nardelli, has been working on the idea for longer. The people asked not to be identified because the information isn’t public. Bloomberg first reported Ek’s backing of Europa Fusion.

Nardelli has extensive experience in the AI world, having previously worked at Google DeepMind, Facebook AI Research, and Microsoft Research Cambridge. Recently, he was head of machine learning at Pacific Fusion, the fusion power startup based in Fremont, California.

Pacific Fusion recently broke ground on a demonstration facility in New Mexico. Like most fusion startups of its size, Pacific Fusion leans heavily on physical devices to test its theories and improve its simulations.

Nardelli apparently didn’t agree with the focus on hardware, instead thinking that fusion startups should invest in a more simulation- and data-heavy approach, a source familiar with his thinking told TechCrunch.

Like Pacific Fusion, Europa Fusion will pursue a pulsed-power approach to fusion. Pulsed-power reactors use massive, perfectly timed surges of electrical current to compress fuel targets until the atoms inside fuse and release energy.

Fusion power has the potential to be a significant energy source in the coming decades, but to date, no one has built a power plant capable of supplying electricity to the grid. If fusion power becomes commercially viable, though, it could possibly be a significant source of carbon-free power that draws on nearly limitless fuel supplies. The technology also has the potential to alleviate concerns about AI’s energy consumption.

Fusion’s ability to generate electricity without exacerbating climate change apparently drove Nardelli’s pivot from AI.

“My original background is in electronics and control engineering,” he wrote on his website. “I used to build robots for a living. One day I’ll be back to that, but it’s time to fix climate change for good.”

Tim De Chant is a senior climate reporter at TechCrunch. He has written for a wide range of publications, including Wired magazine, the Chicago Tribune, Ars Technica, The Wire China, and NOVA Next, where he was founding editor.

De Chant is also a lecturer in MIT’s Graduate Program in Science Writing, and he was awarded a Knight Science Journalism Fellowship at MIT in 2018, during which time he studied climate technologies and explored new business models for journalism. He received his PhD in environmental science, policy, and management from the University of California, Berkeley, and his BA degree in environmental studies, English, and biology from St. Olaf College.

#201. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

#198. Experts warn: Nearly everyone in AI is susceptible to a honey-trap scheme right now

Even as the fall-out for Nvidia over Jensen Huang’s alleged honey-trap scandal is still unfolding, Silicon Valley has yet to reckon with a new reality: many more people among them are at risk.

“Nobody briefs founders on counterintelligence,” Aaron Brown, a former senior CIA officer and current founder of the software company Lumbra, told TechCrunch.

That’s because historically honey traps have been associated with politicians, the military, and national intelligence — the people “behind clearances and a workforce trained to expect an approach,” from spies and other attackers.

But now many of the most valuable secrets “sit inside companies building chips, models, and infrastructure,” Brown says, adding that a spy “wants what the CEO can touch. The roadmap, the export decisions, the customer list, who gets the technology. The CEO is the shortest path to all of it.”

Huang has been accused of falling for just such a honey trap by having an affair with Alice Huang. She’s a former executive of a Singapore neocloud who’s been accused of being a Chinese spy and of smuggling billions of dollars’ worth of restricted Nvidia chips into China.

After the Huang allegations broke, jokes flooded the internet about which tech CEO will be next, but it’s not just CEOs and founders who are at risk. The WSJ reported that OpenAI started warning its employees about outsiders trying to cozy up to them in search of company secrets. Anyone with access to sensitive information — from board members to executive assistants — can be the target of a honey trap.

And typically, the attackers aren’t lovers. Friendship is actually the oldest honey-trap scheme around, Brown said.

Honey traps gain access to their targets as investors, customers, recruiters, and consultancies. Their requests look legitimate on paper and don’t need their targets to fall in love. “They need you to feel comfortable talking,” Brown said. “They last longer because nobody’s looking.”

What’s interesting, too, is the irony. So many tech founders are moving to automate and digitize so much of human life and yet remain vulnerable to the oldest bug around, a person who preys on the human need to be understood by others.

“Why fight through a firewall when you can get close to the person who can open doors, influence decisions, or make requests nobody wants to question?” Perry Carpenter, a criminologist and chief deception strategist at KnowBe4, told TechCrunch.

Once targets realize they have been compromised, the threat can escalate: attackers now have a means of blackmail.

Another irony: AI has made honey-trapping much easier and cheaper. Planning a scheme used to take months or years of research, crafting a persona, knowing the target, and executing a plan. These days, it’s easy to use AI to research a target, generate fake social profiles, fake backgrounds, even fake voice calls.

“AI can help sustain an entire fictional backstory,” Carpenter said. “The technology helps set the stage, but the deception ultimately plays out through human relationships.”

The AI industry “became a national security industry faster than its culture caught up,” Brown said.

“Part of being honey-trapped is not knowing,” he added. “It doesn’t feel like manipulation. It feels genuine, even lucky.”

Carpenter said a good first step is looking for unusual patterns. Note, for instance, “coincidental encounters that happen a little too often,” he said. There are also obvious signs, like persistent curiosity about work and colleagues, or requesting secrecy about meeting up, and pressing for introductions or information.

Bigger red flags include if someone’s requests are getting bigger, pushing the boundaries of what you usually would be uncomfortable doing. “A simple gut check,” Brown said. “Would this person still be around if you lost your job tomorrow?”

It also helps to try and understand when emotions might be influencing a decision. Carpenter suggests putting up a clear boundary between one’s personal and professional life. Within companies, it also helps to “eliminate executive exceptionalism,” he said, the idea that the rules don’t apply to people in power at the company.

Brown added that company culture plays another role, too: Are employees comfortable with raising concerns, challenging unusual requests, and escalating possible threats? “One of the most dangerous security cultures is one where nobody feels comfortable questioning the CEO,” Carpenter said.

“Founders need to take risks, and they need to explore business opportunities,” Brown said. “Paranoia doesn’t help. Recognizing you’re a target does.”

Dominic-Madori Davis is a senior venture capital and startup reporter at TechCrunch. She is based in New York City.

#196. Germany news: Support for Ukraine military aid waning, survey shows

Nearly half of Germany's population wants to reduce military aid to Ukraine, a survey has found. Meanwhile, German consumers are set to pay more in energy bills next year due in part to the Iran war. DW has more.

This is a roundup of the top stories from and about Germany on the weekend of Saturday, November 21, and Sunday, November 22, 2026:

Borussia Dortmund midfielder Felix Nmecha led his team to the top of the German Bundesliga table with a 1-0 victory over last-place Borussia Mönchengladbach.

Meanwhile, Bayern Munich was held to a 1-1 draw at home by Cologne on Friday, and striker Harry Kane suffered a serious ankle injury. The Ballon d'Or winner is set to miss several months after undergoing ankle surgery earlier on Saturday.

Now, Dortmund has 24 points after 10 games, putting them two points ahead of Bayern and third-place Freiburg, who defeated Elversberg 1-0.

Fourth-place RB Leipzig had five different players score in a 5-1 victory over Union Berlin, bringing them to 21 points. Bayer Leverkusen scored four unanswered goals against Paderborn to stay in fifth place.

Germany is seeking to redress years of underinvestment by pouring billions into the modernization of public infrastructure and digital networks, and the transition to renewable energy sources.

But will the plan bring real change amid the labor shortages, burdensome bureaucracy and low productivity currently besetting Europe's largest economy?

Bundesliga powerhouse Bayern Munich concluded the 2025-26 financial year with record figures.

According to the financial report released Saturday at the club's annual general meeting, the club's total turnover was €988.2 million ($1.08 billion) — that is around €10 million more than the previous year.

Operating profit (EBITDA) increased from €187.8 million to €190.8 million. Pre-tax profit was €35.3 million, and net profit for the year was €22.8 million.

Bayern had a particularly strong year in terms of sponsorships. Turnover increased by nearly €30 million, reaching €268.4 million. The club also set a record in merchandise sales, earning €152.8 million.

"We have achieved another record year," CEO Jan-Christian Dreesen said. "This shows that our club has a strong foundation. And we continue to invest to ensure the best possible conditions for the sporting success of our teams."

Moreover, Bayern achieved another milestone. They reached 500,000 members — more than any other club in the world.

Over the past 12 years, the German giants have doubled their membership, growing from 250,000 in 2014 to 500,000 this year.

"Record champions, record members, record member champions," president Herbert Hainer said.

Members hold a 70% stake in the club, which is well above the simple majority required by German rules.

The remaining 30% is owned by four German companies with roots in Bavaria: Adidas, Audi, Allianz, and Viessmann.

Bayern won the league and cup double in the 2025-26 season, losing only three of 53 games across all competitions. In Europe, the club made it to the Champions League semifinals.

An online portal set up to collate records of right-wing violence in Germany since World War II is to lose its funding from the start of next year, a parliamentary inquiry has revealed.

In response to a question from Green politician Marlene Schönberger, the minister of state for culture, Wolfram Weimer, said that "[a]dditional funds for financing beyond the end of 2026 are not available" for the online hub, hosted on the "Archivportal-D" of the German Digital Library.

Schönberger, a member of the Bundestag's Committee on Internal Affairs, said she could not understand the decision by the government to cease funding for the portal.

"Not even eight months after the launch of the 'Right-Wing Violence' thematic portal, the federal government is now pulling the plug. That is completely incomprehensible," she said.

"The federal government must prevent right-wing violence, and where it fails to do so, it must at least acknowledge and document its continuity. It owes that to those affected," she said.

The hub was set up to bring together documents held in various archives in one online location.

#194. Startup sues Apple in India over tap-to-share contacts feature

Apple is being sued in India by a U.S.-based startup that alleges the iPhone maker’s NameDrop feature, which lets users share contact information by bringing devices close together, infringes its patented technology.

GG Technologies, the startup behind StayTouch, an app that lets users exchange contact details by bringing their phones close together, is seeking ₹15 billion (about $161.86 million) in damages and an injunction against Apple, per court documents reviewed by TechCrunch.

Santa Monica-headquartered GG Technologies filed the lawsuit in India in October after mediation proceedings initiated in April failed in September, the startup told TechCrunch. Pre-institution mediation is generally required before filing commercial lawsuits in India. The company also sued Apple in the U.S. in April over related patent infringement claims.

In its 781-page court filing in the Bombay High Court, GG Technologies alleged that Apple’s NameDrop infringes its Indian patent on technology that helps phones exchange contact details with nearby devices without requiring manual pairing. The company launched StayTouch in 2019, the same year it first sought patent protection for the technology in the U.S. It filed its Indian patent application in 2021 and received the patent in July 2024.

Apple introduced NameDrop as part of iOS 17 in June 2023. The feature debuted on iPhones later that year, with support for compatible Apple Watches arriving in October through watchOS 10.1.

“We are seeking an injunction to restrict the usage of the NameDrop feature which is native to iOS,” GG Technologies told TechCrunch. GG Technologies urged the court in its filing to restrict Apple from selling or importing iPhones and Apple Watches preloaded with NameDrop in India. The case has yet to be listed for a hearing.

In the U.S., the International Trade Commission opened an investigation into Apple’s alleged patent infringement in August, following a complaint from GG Technologies. The startup is seeking an order that could block imports of infringing Apple devices into the country, along with restrictions on their sale.

Founded in 2018 by Gaurav Goel, a graduate of the Indian Institute of Technology (IIT) Delhi and a former Goldman Sachs investment banker, GG Technologies developed StayTouch as an alternative to exchanging business cards. The app is available on iOS and Android.

Apple did not respond to a request for comment.

India has become increasingly important to Apple, both as a fast-growing consumer market and a major manufacturing hub outside China. The Cupertino company has expanded its retail presence in the South Asian nation, where iPhones still account for less than 10% of smartphone shipments, giving considerable room for growth.

#192. Alaska, your next governor is a Wikipedia nerd

We have arrived at a moment in American politics when people who are young enough to have grown up on the internet are being elected to office. That means there’s a lot more snooping into politicians’ online pasts to come. This month’s elections were no exception.

Earlier this month, 37-year-old Democrat Jonathan Kreiss-Tomkins won the election for governor of Alaska, making him the first person to hold the office who was born in Alaska after it became a state in 1959. But when people went looking for more information about the Alaskan governor-elect, they didn’t find ill-advised tweets or compromising photos — they found out that he used to be an extremely active Wikipedia editor.

On a Reddit page dedicated to discussing Wikipedia, someone posted that they had found what seemed very likely to be Kreiss-Tomkins’ account — a page for a user named Jonathan.s.kt from Sitka, Alaska who went to Yale, liked mountaineering, and made 13,358 Wikipedia edits between 2005 and 2011. The governor-elect is also from Alaska, went to Yale, and climbs mountains.

Wikipedia editors are an impressive community: They keep one of the most important sources of information online (mostly) clean and accurate. This only makes Kreiss-Tomkins look better as a legislator. Check this guy out! A dedicated public servant to the global constituency of Wikipedia! But a moderator on the Wikipedia subreddit pointed out that we cannot be 100% certain that this account is his.

“The odds that somebody would spend six years creating articles about Sitka, Alaska, Yale, and uploading PD photos of mountains, all to deceive people into thinking they were a disenchanted Howard Dean volunteer who was from Sitka, attended Yale, and was a mountaineer, is effectively zero. Plus they uploaded a photo of themself that looks like every other photo of JKT, minus the bald part,” one commenter replied.

Fair enough. If someone created a Wikipedia account pretending to be some guy in Alaska, made over 13,000 Wikipedia edits, and then waited two decades until that guy became the governor of America’s 49th state in order to sabotage him by making him look like a conscientious denizen of the web, kudos to them. But I’m a journalist. I have literal nightmares about getting stuff wrong. If you’re not careful, it’s easy to get duped.

So I emailed Kreiss-Tomkins, who is probably fielding many more important messages right now about policy, and asked if this is, in fact, his Wikipedia account.

So, there you have it, people of Alaska — your next governor is a nerd.

#189. Is selling an AI version of your face to this microdrama startup the future of Hollywood?

For the most part, the content offered by the microdrama startup Shortical is indistinguishable from the rest of its industry. The company produces soapy, melodramatic storylines — almost always risqué romances — that, in bite-sized two-minute episodes, compel viewers to stay glued to their phones so as not to miss the latest twist in a tawdry, cliff-hanger-happy saga.

The primary way that Shortical — which was founded last January — makes money is through subscriptions. Typically, the first 7-10 episodes of a micro-drama are free. After that, if the user wants to finish the series, they have to pay a small monthly fee which unlocks access to all of the site’s content.

Over the past year, the company has churned out a gush of popular live-action romances like “Crossing the Line with My Neighbor,” “Accidentally Married My Boss,” and “Spoiled by My Billionaire Sugar Daddy.”

Lately, however, the company’s homepage is covered in AI-generated content. The reason isn’t exactly hard to figure out: the production overhead for AI content is substantially less than live-action. The profit potential, meanwhile, is substantially higher, if you can draw an audience.

Its latest microdrama premiered in Los Angeles last week. The series, called “Inevitable,” stars Aki Avni, an Israeli film and television star who has been visible in American TV shows for years (he had a stint on Fox’s 9/11-era thriller “24” years ago) but is now trying something largely unheard of in modern Hollywood: licensing his face to Shortical for the sake of its AI content.

The project, which is wholly AI, used Avni’s face to create a digital avatar of him. In “Inevitable,” he plays himself – in a very meta plot in which he — the actor Aki Avni — licenses his face to a film studio, after which thriller-like antics ensue.

This is Shortical’s first foray into such a licensing deal, but it likely won’t be their last.

In a phone interview, Avni told TechCrunch that he found the project both nerveracking and exciting. “Look, it’s a new era for artists,” the actor said. “AI is a new kind of art. It’s a new format. It’s always great to be the pioneer in something.”

Guy Shimoni, the CEO of Shortical, emphasized the creative potential of the new tool. “One of the things that AI does is it enables smaller teams to be able to produce these movie-scale productions,” Shimoni said. “When we were doing mobile-first live action, we were limited by budget, but now we can serve them with epic fantasies. We have kingdoms and dragons. We have superheroes. We have werewolves.”

But AI-generated romances involving werewolves — who, exactly, is watching this kind of thing?

Apparently, a lot of people. While China boasts the biggest microdrama audience globally, Shimoni says that most of his platform’s viewers are actually in the U.S. The industry’s viewer demographic skews heavily female. Many viewers may watch them to wind down at night, getting minor dopamine hits as they drift off to sleep. Many of the AI-generated programs that already exist on the company’s website appear to have millions of views.

Now, Shortical is betting on the creation of a new generation of creators — those who, like Avni, are willing to surrender their likenesses for compensation. “LICENSE YOUR FACE” Shortical’s website advertises, explaining that interested parties can submit their social media handles to the company, which will then decide whether to offer them “a contract setting out the terms” of using their persona in the company’s AI-generated content.

A company rep told TechCrunch that this “offering is still in its early days and hasn’t yet resulted in any signed contracts.” They added: “The series with Aki is the first time Shortical has used a real person’s likeness, and it was done through a direct agreement with him rather than through the platform. Shortical hopes the series will draw more attention to face licensing and plans to promote the offering more actively following this launch.”

Much of this trend has already taken hold in China where the microdrama industry originated, where AI-generated content is big, and where face-licensing is already a trend. Although the trend may not be a particularly profitable one for creators. A recent report in Rest of World found that Microdrama firms were paying people “between US$15 to US$700 to license their facial likenesses.”

How much money is Shortical offering for its face-licensing deals? The company isn’t saying. As for if Shortical will offer royalty-based compensation for creators — like the residual system in television, “It’s a case by case [basis],” Shimoni answers vaguely.

A Shortical representative further explained to TechCrunch that the company didn’t want to “share a specific figure” for Avni’s particular contract. However, the person said, he came “on as a partner in the project, and his compensation was in line with industry standards for a comparable non-AI acting role.”

For other actors, however, the deals may be quite different. Plus, many creatives are understandably unsettled by signs of automation creeping into their industry. Until recently, microdramas seemed like a safe, entry-level gateway into the entertainment industry, where young actors could gain experience on sets and hone their craft. Now, companies are downsizing and producing content that doesn’t even require real actors.

Jennifer Cooper, a longtime fan of the industry and founder of the consultancy firm Vertical Drama Love, notes that there’s a lot of distrust of AI — both from audiences and within the microdrama industry itself.

“Everyone’s been impacted by AI,” she said. “A lot of us have either lost work to AI or can see that it’s devastated this industry — [taking microdramas] from something that was a rare thing in the world, that was in growth, and providing work, particularly for young people…[and now] you know, it’s been decimated.”

Questions of consent with AI likenesses have also dogged the industry (some creators claim they have had their likenesses stolen), which is something that Shortical is obviously trying to address with its new face-licensing endeavor.

Yet these licensing deals could also help to gig-ify the industry, creating a scenario in which creators pay a distributor for the chance to platform their content (or their face), then receive payouts based on the audience engagement they’re allowed to generate. Shortical recently partnered with a company called Payouts.com, which describes itself as a “payout infrastructure platform powering creator and gig economy disbursements at scale.” The plan is to create a system by which creatives can seamlessly interact with Shortical’s platform and get paid.

Cooper believes the push towards AI in the industry is driven by broader financial trends. Most of the microdrama business is led by startups, and VCs love to hear a growth game plan involving automation. “For anyone to get investment at the moment in microdramas, they’ve got to mention the word AI,” she says. “It’s like AI equals gold in everyone’s eyes.”

Despite all the investment in this space, Cooper seems skeptical that there is actually a huge audience for this kind of content. “Some of it does get views. A lot of it doesn’t,” she said, of the AI content. “I think it’s being pushed on an audience more than there is an audience demand or wish for it.”
