#188. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#185. Here are the top AI agents that can live in your text messages

Rather than downloading another app, a growing number of agents can simply be texted like an ordinary person.

You text it what you need, and it can remember context, connect to the apps and services you already use, and complete tasks on your behalf. That can mean scheduling an appointment, organizing a calendar, researching a trip, sending an email, making a reservation, shopping online, or reminding you about something days later.

While Instinct is one of the buzziest AI agents at present, thanks to its $10 billion valuation after its latest funding round of $1 billion, there are many others also making a play for this space.

Below are the most notable options so far, from general-purpose personal assistants to agents designed for families, travel, and work.

Ask Proxy’s biggest differentiator is that it “thinks like a woman,” meaning it aims to anticipate your needs before you even ask, the company explains on its website.

It works like a chatbot you can text like a friend, whether you need gift ideas, help planning an event, or recommendations for things to do that weekend. You can ask it to add important dates to your calendar, set reminders, or help with everyday decisions. You can also send it photos, like an empty table, to figure out how to decorate the table for a dinner party.

Unlike a typical chatbot, Ask Proxy also takes a proactive approach. It can reach out when it notices something you might forget or when it comes across a new activity, restaurant, or experience you might enjoy.

There’s also a work-focused version, which the company explains “gets employees back to work faster, while keeping personal data off company systems.”

The personal version costs $1 per day. For businesses, pricing starts at $15 per employee per month for large companies and $23 per employee per month for small teams with fewer than 49 employees.

Ask Proxy works over SMS. You can text “start” to 820-732-2625.

Caddy is an AI assistant that turns the information scattered across your phone into things you can actually act on. It lives in iMessage for iPhone users and RCS for Android users, so there’s no separate app or inbox to constantly check.

For instance, an email contains an appointment that needs to be added to your calendar or a friend sends a list of things to pick up. Instead of worrying about all the details getting buried, Caddy connects to your calendars and conversations, then identifies things that may require action. It can add events to your calendar, set reminders, keep track of follow-ups, and even do research for you.

Caddy has been available in public beta since April 2026.

Comma is designed to manage tasks across both work and everyday life. Comma carries tasks through to completion, checking its own work and determining when a task is finished.

Completed work is surfaced for review when a decision or approval is needed. It also proactively notifies users and recommends the next steps.

It can work across a user’s browser, computer, and files, while remaining accessible remotely through messaging apps such as Signal, Telegram, and WeChat. Comma maintains context and memory across devices and conversations, and can deliver daily briefings, flag urgent matters, and summarize recorded calls.

Fambot is an AI-powered “chief of staff” for families, designed to bring together the many moving parts of family life, including school communications, sports activities, meal planning, calendars, and other day-to-day responsibilities, and turn them into an organized plan.

The service is designed to work across both an app and text messages. Fambot is available on iOS devices, Android devices, and the web, and families can also interact with it directly over SMS. Every night, Fambot automatically sends a summary of the following day, including upcoming events, to-dos, and details such as school uniform or packing requirements. Parents can reply to those messages to ask questions, check information, or make changes to their calendar.

Fambot currently connects to Gmail, Google Calendar, Outlook, and WhatsApp, with support for Apple Calendar planned for the future.

The company launched in beta in early September 2026 and has raised $3.5 million in pre-seed funding. The service is currently free during the beta period, but Fambot expects to eventually charge roughly the equivalent of a Netflix subscription.

Folk is designed to be an AI assistant you can reach through iMessage, WhatsApp, and Telegram. It can remember personal context, manage reminders, research topics, track flights, handle email, and perform real-world tasks such as restaurant reservations.

One distinction is that Folk runs on its own private cloud computer. It can also run code and execute multi-step tasks rather than merely responding conversationally.

Folk launched its beta in May 2026, and it’s available for free but also offers a Pro subscription for $8.33/month if you want unlimited tasks running in the background.

Haven distinguishes itself by presenting the concept of a “digital twin” rather than merely serving as a text-based AI assistant.

According to the company, Haven understands and adapts to your preferences, routines, and decision-making styles, creating a personalized memory graph based on your interactions. As the company describes, “A personal assistant knows what you asked it to do. Haven knows what you would do and how you would do it.”

Notably, the company envisions future features that customize your experiences across devices, like TV and car.

Currently, Haven is available for free in beta on iPhone and Android devices, and the web. You can also reach Haven by calling or texting (435) 260-6500.

Instinct first made headlines after raising $350 million at a $2.5 billion valuation. In September 2026, it raised $1 billion, valuing it at $10 billion.

The always-available agent can take actions on your behalf, rather than simply answer questions. Users can communicate with Instinct by text or voice and connect it to the apps and services they already use, such as email, calendar, Google Workspace, and more. The company says early users have used Instinct for tasks ranging from planning trips and buying groceries to booking tickets and canceling subscriptions.

Powered by Hermes Agent, Iris is designed to let users delegate tasks to an AI assistant directly through iMessage. Users can connect the apps they use and hand over a task with a simple message. The agent then works through the required steps, while retaining relevant context from previous interactions. It’s also designed to follow up with users proactively rather than waiting for another request.

Martin is a general-purpose personal assistant reachable through SMS as well as phone, WhatsApp, email, Slack, and its own iOS app.

It can help manage calendars, email, reminders, tasks, notes, and communications. It can also act on behalf of users by sending messages or making phone calls, while providing daily summaries of what needs attention.

It’s a bit on the pricier side, with subscriptions starting at $21 per month.

Maverick describes itself as an “AI companion who lives in your texts,” acting as a personal assistant that helps manage everyday tasks directly from your messages.

It can fix typos, cancel subscriptions, schedule meetings from voice notes, and help group chats settle on a date for an event. One notable feature is that Maverick asks for your permission before taking action.

The platform also offers hundreds of integrations, including Discord, Gmail, and Slack, allowing it to connect with the apps you already use.

It’s currently in beta and free to use on iMessage. There’s also a Pro version for €27 per month (or approximately $30.25 USD), which includes 500 tasks a month,  20 connected apps, scheduled tasks, and memory.

Miso takes the personal-assistant concept and applies it specifically to travel.

It operates through iMessage and combines AI trip planning with support from a dedicated travel team. Users can arrange flights through messaging while Miso takes into account their travel preferences, loyalty points, and other priorities. Miso also gives travelers access to trip details and flight updates, along with personalized recommendations.

Ohai is another family assistant focused on helping households manage the constant stream of information and tasks involved in everyday family life.

Users can send messages, forward emails, or make voice requests, and the assistant can transform them into schedules, reminders, and plans. It can also coordinate calendars, household chores, and meals across multiple members of a family.

It includes a free basic option, but also offers different subscriptions based on family size, starting at $9.99 per month.

Orbits also has an AI assistant that is focused on the household.

It works through both the Orbits app and text messaging, and can bring together calendars, lists, and conversations while also carrying out tasks for you. That can include searching the web, making purchases, booking restaurants, changing appointments, and requesting quotes for household services.

Additionally, Orbits can remind family members about chores, communicate with service providers, and help coordinate responsibilities beyond the immediate household.

Orbits is backed by investors like Andreessen Horowitz (a16z) Speedrun fund, N49P, and Garage Capital.

Users can interact with Pally as they would with a friend while giving it access to the apps they already rely on. The assistant can connect information spread across services such as WhatsApp, Gmail, calendars, and Google Drive, helping users keep track of plans, receipts, dates, files, and other details.

There’s a free plan available. It works over iMessage, RCS, and Telegram.

Poke launched in March 2026 as a general-purpose assistant that you can reach through text messaging. It can handle tasks involving calendars, planning, health and fitness, smart-home controls, photos, and other everyday activities.

Poke became particularly notable in June 2026 when Apple approved Poke as the first AI agent on the Apple Messages for Business platform. A month later, parent company The Interaction Company of California was acquired by AI coding startup Cognition in a deal valued in the low nine figures.

Rene is an AI personal assistant designed to be a user’s “best contact” rather than another productivity agent. It’s text-based and can turn natural-language conversations into tasks, reminders, summaries, and other follow-up actions. Rene also maintains context across conversations, allowing it to remember details and surface things that may need attention later.

Additionally, it has a browser, can write code, shop for you, as well as create slides and images.

Rene is currently available on iMessage, Telegram, and WhatsApp.

Instead of waiting for users to open an app, Skye is an “agentic homescreen” for the iPhone that analyzes information from connected services and surfaces what it thinks is relevant through contextual cards, suggestions, and daily briefings. The assistant can generate audio briefings, prepare information ahead of meetings, surface reminders, and identify potentially useful information.

The company, Signull Labs, raised about $3.6 million in seed funding in 2025. It entered private testing and beta in April 2026, and hasn’t yet announced a public release date.

Stanley is an AI content assistant aimed at content creators rather than general personal productivity. Available through iMessage, Telegram, and the web, it acts as a kind of AI head of content, helping users come up with ideas, turn them into posts, and maintain a consistent publishing schedule.

For instance, Stanley can connect to a user’s Instagram account, using that context to suggest content based on what the user has actually been doing. Users can also send voice notes, which Stanley turns into scripts, captions, and other social assets. It includes a content calendar and can schedule posts, with the broader goal of making content creation a more continuous, automated process rather than something users have to sit down and plan from scratch.

Szn is designed to coordinate tasks and follow-ups through a single conversation thread. Users can send text messages, voice notes, photos, or forwarded emails, and the assistant takes action on their behalf, including making calls, sending emails, and managing follow-ups.

According to the company’s website, Szn has served more than 7,500 businesses across 16 countries, completed over 10,000 jobs, and handled 95 hours of calls.

Tab allows users to delegate everyday tasks through familiar messaging platforms. Users can send requests to the assistant via iMessage or WhatsApp, ranging from ordering groceries to finding birthday gifts, and Tab handles the process on their behalf.

The company emerged from stealth in early October with a valuation of $300 million. Its investors include SV Angel, Valar Ventures, and American Spirit.

The biggest differentiator with Tomo’s personal assistant for iMessage is that, instead of being a private assistant that only you interact with, Tomo can become part of group conversations.

Also, like other agents, it’s designed to help with goals, accountability, scheduling, and everyday organization. It also has the ability to send reminders, search the web, work with calendars and email, watch videos, and edit photos.

The basic plan starts at $19.99 per month.

Town is aimed more at professional life. It gives users a “Townie,” an AI assistant designed to learn how they work and handle recurring professional tasks. It can work with email, calendars, documents, Slack, and other platforms.

In June 2026, the company raised a $55 million Series A led by Andreessen Horowitz and Forerunner.

The company plans to offer the app for free initially and has stated that it will never include advertising.

Wajo’s first consumer agent, Fo, can call businesses, send emails, join group chats, make bookings and purchases, and coordinate with people to complete everyday tasks.

A key part of Wajo’s approach is giving Fo its own identity. The agent has its own email address, phone number, and payment card, allowing it to interact with businesses without requiring users to hand over their own credentials.

Notably, when Fo encounters something it cannot handle on its own, Wajo says it can bring in a human assistant to finish the task.

Fo launched publicly in September 2026 with a free tier, while heavier workloads and custom agents are available through a paid Pro offering.

#183. Anthropic researcher uses AI to solve math's greatest riddle

The proposed breakthrough could reshape mathematical research, but still needs independent verification. It's the latest in a string of cases where AI has outpaced humans in solving famous maths problems.

A mathematician working for AI giant Anthropic says he used the company's Claude AI tool to solve one of mathematics most famous problems.

The Riemann hypothesis dates back to 1859 and centers on the distribution of prime numbers underpinning a much wider scope of mathematical work.

Levent Alpoge, a member of Anthropic's technical staff, made the announcement on his website. "I believe I have, working with Claude, discovered a proof of the Riemann Hypothesis," Levent Alpoge wrote in a note released Friday.

The proof still has to be verified and confirmed by other mathematicians. If it withstands independent examination, it would be a major milestone both in mathematics and the growing role of artificial intelligence (AI) in scientific research.

The Riemann hypothesis is the latest and most important in a string of famous mathematical problems that were tackled with the help of AI.

Alpoge said that the collaboration with AI had generated more than 250,000 pages of mathematical material, adding that he had not yet mastered all the mathematics involved.

"Claude and I have had a great time together with such breadth in recent months, but this time Claude far outpaced me near the finish line," he wrote. "No, Claude flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line."

The Riemann Hypothesis is one of seven major mathematical problems, the so-called Millennium Prize Problems, selected by the Clay Mathematics Institute, which offers $1 million for a solution to each of those.

"This is an announcement of historic significance," Martin Bridson, president of the institute, said in a statement quoted by news agency AP.

He added it "holds the promise of a dramatic transformation in the perception of what is possible in the struggle to extend the human understanding of mathematics."

"But the journey from today’s announcement to a place of real understanding will be long and difficult."

Formulated by German mathematician Bernhard Riemann in the 19th century, the hypothesis is about the distribution of prime numbers, which are numbers that can only be divided by themselves and the number 1.

The hypothesis describes a fundamental pattern of how these numbers are distributed as they become ever larger. Confirming that hypothesis is thought to have crucial implications for a wide range of further mathematical research.

The potential breakthrough by Alpoge and Claude AI comes amid a growing number of examples where artificial intelligence systems are making significant contributions to advanced mathematical research.

In September, rival AI company OpenAI said its technology had helped solve a problem involving the Navier-Stokes equations.

The equations, which describe the movement of fluids such as water and air, are another of the seven Millennium Prize Problems, but the claim that they've been solved also still needs independent verification.

#175. SNL’s skit Sister captures the quiet agony of caregiving for women: ‘I’ve never felt seen like that’

The viral satirization of the caregiving burden left to women and daughters hit home for many viewers

The Saturday Night Live sketch, titled Sister quickly went viral this week for its satirization of the burden that women and daughters face to take care of aging family members. In the parody commercial, comedian John Mulaney touts the benefit of “Sister”, who he describes as “a live-in 24/7 elder care provider that is completely free, and also your sister” . “Now I don’t have to worry about dad getting the care and attention he needs because my sister will do it, and I can get back to my life, and the stuff I like to do,” Mulaney says, as the sister, played by cast member Jane Wickline, gives their father’s medication to him.

The sketch, which amassed over seven million views on YouTube in less than a week since it aired, has received thousands of responses and comments about its accuracy. “This is one of the MOST wicked and spot on SNL sketches ever written,” wrote one commenter on YouTube. Another posted: “As a hospice nurse this definitely tracks. I deal 90% of the time with adult daughters, and if there is no daughter, it is the daughter in law.” “This is so real it almost gave me a stroke from rage,” added another viewer.

The parody even garnered the attention of major US leaders, including Melinda French Gates and US senator Kirsten Gillibrand. “I don’t know of any [SNL] sketch that has so quickly made its way into so many group chats as the [“Sister”] commercial,” French Gates wrote in an Instagram post about the sketch. “I’m so glad we’re talking about this, and laughing about it. We need more honesty about what caring for loved ones really takes and why so much of that work falls to women.”

Gillibrand used the parody ad to tout her own legislation, the Supporting Our Direct Care Workforce and Family Caregivers Act, which she introduced in June 2026. The bill would provide federal funding to states to help train and retain the care workforce, as well as family caregivers.

Currently, the US has no federal support for family caregivers. Medicare largely covers short-term medical needs, and while Medicaid can cover long-term care, it’s only available if an individual meets the medical and financial criteria. “If you’ve seen – or lived – SNL’s ‘Sister’ sketch, you know how quickly life changes when an aging parent needs care,” Gillibrand wrote on X. “Congress should come together to pass my bill that would give family caregivers the resources and support they need.”

Stacey Barnes was sitting on the couch next to her parents, both asleep, when she saw the sketch as it aired live. The 45-year-old web developer has been living with her parents in Minnesota since her mother was first diagnosed with Parkinson’s a year-and-a-half ago, and currently acts as her primary caretaker.

Barnes was stunned by how accurate the sketch felt. “It just came on TV, and I was like, “Whoa, oh my god!” she said.

“I immediately went to YouTube the next day and sent it to people,” she told the Guardian. “I’ve never felt seen like that before. I’ve never identified with a lot of pop culture. This is the first time some kind of pop culture–maybe since Daria – where I was like, ‘Yeah, that’s relatable.’”

Shauna Sweeney was on the receiving end of at least a dozen messages about the sketch. The 41-year-old took on caregiving responsibilities when her father was diagnosed with early-onset Alzheimer’s in 2014. Her own experience led her to start tendercare, a free caregiving and elder care platform that helps families organize care plans, prepare for emergencies, and connect with local providers.

“My friends all know that I’ve been deeply in this space for a while,” Sweeney said. “But also this is something that many of us who are considered sisters have been acknowledging in private circles for some time.”

More than 51 million Americans – nearly one in five adults – are providing care to someone age 50 and older, according to research from AARP and the National Alliance for Caregiving. Three out of five caregivers are women.

“When my dad was diagnosed, I don’t even think there was a lot of conscious division or discussion around who was going to be responsible for what,” Sweeney said. “It’s just assumed there’s a responsible one in the family [who] is going to do what is required.”

Sweeney said that the SNL sketch shines a light on a growing issue that women are facing across the country, especially as the population ages and the average life expectancy increases: “I didn’t have language for this. I didn’t even know what a caregiver was for the first five years that I was doing the job.”

While not always the case, studies have shown that this burden of care falls disproportionately on women, as depicted in the Sister sketch. A group of researchers even wrote that “the best long-term care insurance in our country is a conscientious daughter” in a 2017 Journal of American Medicine (JAMA) commentary on dementia care.

“A lot of the socialization comes with unspoken rules that encourage [and] sometimes even reward girls for being helpful and accommodating,” said Dr Sarah Oreck, a reproductive psychiatrist and founder of Mavida Health, a virtual mental health provider for women.

The SNL sketch addresses this as well, as a doctor explains to the audience: “Years of internalized sexism warp Sister’s brain into thinking caretaking is her responsibility and somehow not yours.”

Dr Oreck elaborated on the sketch, pointing out that girls are taught from a young age to take care of others: “Girls play with baby dolls and pretend to feed and change the baby.”

The sketch’s virality has landed it in the group chats and inboxes of “Brothers” and “Sisters” across the world, but its long-lasting impact is yet to be determined. Still, caregivers and experts alike agree that it’s doing what good comedy should do: spark conversations.

“Part of what makes [the sketch] funny is also that it’s so tragic,” said Sweeney. “As you see, it’s nearly impossible to do all of this [caregiving] without there being real fallout. Whether it’s to your mental health or financially, the consequences are real.”

#173. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

Death adds to national measles outbreak that recently cost the US its measles elimination status after 26 years

Virginia reported its first measles-associated death of 2026 ⁠on Friday, involving a child who tested positive for the infectious disease, the state’s health department said.

The death adds to a worsening national measles outbreak that recently cost the US its measles elimination status after 26 years.

Pennsylvania, the hardest-hit US state, reported an eighth measles-related death and confirmed 1,631 measles cases as of Friday.

Wisconsin has ⁠also reported two deaths, while New York state recorded its first measles-associated ​death, involving a ​child.

The US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) has confirmed six ​measles-associated deaths so far in 2026, though it does not ​specify the states ‌where the deaths were ​recorded.

A discrepancy between ​state and federal death tallies arose after the CDC, under the Trump administration’s health secretary, Robert F Kennedy Jr, began using National Center for Health Statistics mortality data instead of state figures.

Virginia has reported 220 measles ⁠cases so far in 2026, including 40 among children four years old or younger and 75 among those age five to 12, state ⁠health department data showed as of Thursday.

Of those cases, 179 involved unvaccinated individuals, 39 were associated with people ‌whose vaccination status was ‌unknown and two occurred with vaccinated individuals.

“Virginia has not had a measles-related death ‌since 1978,” said Frederic Bertley, immunologist and chief executive officer of the Center of Science and Industry. Bertley added that the measles virus infection is both alarming and evidence that measles is gaining a foothold and becoming a growing problem for the US.

#168. Anthropic mathematician claims he used Claude to prove 19th-century Riemann hypothesis

Levent Alpöge says it took six weeks for him using the AI tool to solve the math problem involving prime numbers

A mathematician at Anthropic is contending that the San Francisco-based artificial intelligence (AI) company’s software has helped crack one of the most important unsolved problems in mathematics.

Levent Alpöge, a Turkish American mathematician, on Friday posted a note on his personal website saying that Claude, the AI software, had helped prove the Riemann hypothesis. He says that Claude helped develop a solution to the problem during the course of about six weeks.

If Alpöge’s claim is ultimately confirmed, the proof could mark another breakthrough in the use of AI for complicated math problems – even as mathematicians express uneasiness around extremely lucrative private companies dishing out millions of dollars for the technology to solve problems.

“Claude and I have had a great time together with such breadth in recent months, but this time Claude far outpaced me near the finish line,” Alpöge wrote, while anthropomorphizing the AI software. “No, Claude flew, and danced, and sparkled near that finish line.”

Alpöge linked to computer code – written in a language called Lean – that he maintained could verify the findings.

Mathematicians subsequently began working to see if Alpöge’s proof could be confirmed.

Alpöge’s note was published just one day after Anthropic went public on the Nasdaq stock market index. He acknowledged that, writing: “The precise timing is horrible. If I could have, I’d have finished this all much earlier.”

The Riemann hypothesis is a nearly 170-year-old math problem named after Bernhard Riemann, a German mathematician who proposed it in 1859.

The hypothesis deals with prime numbers – whole numbers greater than 1 that can only be divided by themselves and 1. Riemann questioned how often prime numbers appear and how they are distributed along the number line. And he developed a formula for estimating how many prime numbers exist under a specific number.

The Riemann hypothesis is one of the seven Millennium prize math problems selected in 200 by the Clay Mathematics Institute. The institute has offered $1m for each problem solved.

Mathematicians have been attempting to solve this problem for years. The New York Times reported that on Friday, an independent mathematician in Italy released an incomplete proof of the Riemann hypothesis that he said he had been working on for almost 12 years. That mathematician, Alessandro Masullo, said he did not use AI because he did not fully trust the system.

Other mathematicians have expressed discomfort with relying on AI to solve math problems.

In September, Anthropic competitor OpenAI claimed that a non-public version of its AI tools helped solve part of another Millennium prize problem. But it was solved in a way that struck mathematicians and observers in a way many found strange: a near-trillion-dollar private company spent $15m to employ 10,000 AI systems to solve the problem.

Alpöge was working on that separate problem for Anthropic when OpenAI made its announcement.

University of Warwick mathematician James Robinson told the Guardian in September that “it’s frustrating to see these big tech companies burning this fuel up just so that they can show off about how great their latest model is”.

He added: “It seems like immature playground boasting writ large, underpinned by billions of dollars and the potential for significant environmental damage in an age when climate change is probably the biggest challenge we face.”

→ Baca artikel asli

#167. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#164. Vance stops short of endorsing Trump’s claim that Iran was close to nuclear weapon

US vice-president says it’s not his place to second-guess the president, but refrains from endorsing Trump’s claim

At a White House news conference on Friday, JD Vance stopped short of endorsing Donald Trump’s claim that Iran was so close to obtaining a nuclear weapon in February that it was essential for the US to go to war with the Islamic republic.

In the White House briefing room, Trump was asked about reporting this week that he was unhappy with the US vice-president’s statement blaming Republican losses in the midterm elections on the Iran war.

“I didn’t say I was unhappy with it,” Trump said. “I thought the statement was fine, except I would have had one other thing, that if it were asked, I would have said that: ‘Would you do it again?’ And absolutely, the answer is yes, because we cannot allow Iran to have a nuclear weapon.”

However, Trump’s claim that Iran was so close to developing a nuclear weapon this year that it was necessary to go to war, which he has made dozens of times in the past nine months, is undermined by public evidence and contradicts his own previous statements on US strikes against Iranian facilities in 2025.

At a campaign rally in Alaska last month, Trump insisted that he had to join Israel in attacking Iran in February because, “they were two weeks away from having a nuclear weapon”.

That claim appears at odds with what his then director of national intelligence, Tulsi Gabbard, testified to Congress on 25 March 2025, two months into Trump’s second term: that US intelligence agencies reported that Iran had not tried to make a nuclear weapon for more than two decades. The US intelligence community, Gabbard said, “continues to assess Iran is not building a nuclear weapon and supreme leader Khamenei has not authorized the nuclear weapons program he suspended in 2003”.

Despite that assessment, three months later, on 21 June 2025, Trump ordered the US bombing of Iran’s nuclear enrichment facilities. The strikes, Trump told the public that night, “were a spectacular military success. Iran’s key nuclear enrichment facilities have been completely and totally obliterated.”

Eight months after that, however, when Trump announced the start of a full-scale war on Iran early on 28 February 2026, he said in a video statement from Mar-a-Lago the war was necessary to “ensure that Iran does not obtain a nuclear weapon”.

The morning after this month’s midterm elections, Vance said in a statement posted on social media: “The reason we lost is the war.”

“If you’d like to argue it was worth it, that losing the Senate for the next 6 years was worth it, then do so,” he added. “Argue it was a worthwhile cost to pay. Agree or disagree, that’s at least honest. But everyone knows why we lost.”

In the White House briefing room on Friday, after Trump said that Vance should have said the war was necessary, a reporter asked the vice-president whether he would argue that the war in Iran was worth it.

In his response, Vance said it was not his place to second-guess the president, but refrained from actually endorsing Trump’s claim that the war was essential to stop Iran from creating a nuclear weapon.

“Look, what I’ve said is the goal of keeping Iran from getting a nuclear weapon is absolutely what we need to be doing. It’s the biggest state sponsor of terrorism. If they had a nuclear weapon, they would use it as leverage or, God forbid, they would attack and kill thousands, hundreds of thousands, of innocent people,” Vance said. “I think the president is the one who makes the decision, and the president has said he would do it again.”

“My view is that my goal is, and my job is, to support the president of the United States,” the vice-president said.

The vice president’s office did not respond to a request to comment.

#163. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#162. Trump orders contractors to check with White House over any congressional inquiries

Anticipating flood of Democratic requests, US president issues executive order in attempt to stymie oversight efforts

Donald Trump is directing federal contractors and subcontractors to consult with his administration about inquiries they receive from Congress and to withhold any communications that the White House considers privileged, according to an executive order issued late Friday.

The companies must also coordinate with the White House and US agencies on “appropriate responses” to any such requests, the order says. If they fail to comply, the White House warns, their contracts could be terminated and their future work with the federal government suspended.

The order signed late Friday is the administration’s latest effort to resist what are expected to be wide-ranging and aggressive oversight efforts from Democrats when they take over Congress in January. Democrats have made government contracts one of their top investigative targets , noting that federal money has been flowing to companies linked with Trump’s family, particularly at the Pentagon.

It comes as White House officials have already begun taking steps to exert more control over how agencies and officials respond to congressional requests, anticipating there will be a flood of investigations once Democrats take power in January.

On Monday, Will Scharf, the White House counsel, told US agencies that they must seek approval from his office and the White House’s office of legislative affairs before agreeing to appear before Congress. Scharf also suggested in a recent letter to the US representative Robert Garcia, the top Democrat on the House oversight committee, that some congressional investigations may amount to “blatant criminality”.

Garcia earlier this week sent letters directing members of Trump’s family and top administration officials to preserve documents. Scharf sought to dismiss the requests, noting in a letter to Democratic lawmakers that they are not yet in the majority and cannot compel compliance.

In a fact sheet, the White House framed the new requirements as “protecting executive privileges from congressional overreach”, arguing that some congressional leaders have tried to intrude on executive branch privileges by “harassing private companies that contract with the federal government”.

Presidents of both parties have long used executive privilege to resist congressional inquiries, but the claims can set up messy, protracted legal fights as parties dispute whether it applies. Executive privilege allows the president to keep information from the courts, Congress and the public to protect the confidentiality of White House deliberations, but the protection is far from absolute.

Friday’s executive order applies to all contractors and subcontractors, a universe of tens of thousands of businesses.

#161. COP31: UN Climate summit runs into overtime as deal remains elusive

After nearly two weeks of negotiations, countries remain divided on how to fight global warming. A new draft declaration doesn't mention the phaseout of fossil fuels, the main drivers of climate change. DW has more.

Diplomats and delegates from nearly 200 countries are meeting in Antalya, Turkey

A new draft deal omits any mention of how to phase out fossil fuels, in what campaigners call a step 'backwards' and a 'glaring regression'

Like in previous years, the climate summit has been extended until a consensus is reached

These are the major developments from the closing phase of the UN's COP31 Climate Summit in Antalya, Turkey, on Friday, November 20 and Saturday, November 21:

Attacks on climate science and disinformation seem to be delaying key decisions as the UN climate talks draw to a close in Antalya.

Leaders from Africa, one of the continents worst hit by climate change, have spent years lobbying for hundreds of billions of dollars to help adapt to the rising impacts of global warming.

The rest of the world relies on African nations for their abundant natural resources, many of them used in clean energy production.

Yet at COP31, wealthy nations have so far failed to commit to the financing needed to help developing nations make a just transition.

The head of Peru's delegation at COP31, Andres Martin Garrido Sanchez, warned that the UN climate summit was unlikely to end with a concrete deal on climate financing or cutting fossil fuel use.

Garrido Sanchez lamented that the outcome "would be very limited."

He reiterated how "geopolitical divisions" were stopping any final deal from mentioning reducing reliance on oil, gas and coal.

The Peruvian delegate singled out major oil producers, such as Gulf Arab states and Russia, as showing significant opposition.

He noted that while Latin American neighbor Panama had also signaled objection to including the term fossil fuels in the final text, Peru was "not so radical" and is keen to attract investment to "ensure our energy security" through renewables.

China has backed a plan proposed by COP host Turkey to raise global electricity demand to 35% by 2035 — up from the current 20%.

In a joint statement with Australia, which is leading negotiations between almost 200 nations at the COP31 summit in Turkey, Beijing said it welcomes "growing international ambition and cooperation ... to increase electricity's share of global final energy consumption to 35 per cent by 2035 on a voluntary basis."

The statement goes on to highlight the benefits the transition can bring for the economy, jobs, energy security, public health and households.

The two partners also "acknowledge the importance of inclusive climate action that ensures no one is left behind," the text continued.

Dozens of countries, including EU states, Japan, South Korea and Canada, have so far signaled support for the voluntary goal, which aims to boost clean energy use.

Yet, environmental groups have warned that the draft text doesn't stipulate the use of solar, wind and other renewable energy sources, which could allow power plants using natural gas and coal to be included.

Negotiators from nearly 200 countries worked through the night to finalize a deal, after a draft text dropped all mention of fossil fuels — the leading cause of climate change.

Negotiations are expected to continue into the weekend. We are pausing our live coverage for now and will bring you further updates as talks progress.

While Turkey hosts COP31 in Antalya, a growing climate threat is unfolding on its Mediterranean beaches. Rising sand temperatures are causing most newly hatched sea turtles to be born female, raising concerns about the long-term survival of local populations.

Conservationists warn that climate change, combined with pollution, coastal development and fishing-related injuries, is putting increasing pressure on the species.

The UN climate summit in Antalya is drawing criticism from activists and campaigners who question whether global climate talks can deliver meaningful change while fossil fuel interests remain influential. Turkey and Australia, both under scrutiny for their energy policies, are leading this year's conference.

Activists, including Greta Thunberg, argue COP summits have become increasingly ineffective, while others defend them as a vital space to pressure governments. Climate policy experts say the talks have helped reduce projected warming, but stress that real progress depends on action taken by countries between summits, not just agreements reached at them.

Heavy rainfall has forced people from their homes, warmer oceans are damaging coral reefs, and changes in fish stocks are putting pressure on the country's fishing industry. Rising sea levels pose another threat to the low-lying island nation. Meanwhile, global greenhouse gas emissions continue to rise, despite more than three decades of international climate negotiations.

At COP31 in Antalya, Turkey, the Maldives' climate minister Ali Sharif discusses why progress has been so slow, what really happens during climate negotiations, and why moving away from fossil fuels is not as straightforward as it might seem.

For island nations like the Maldives, switching to renewable energy comes with practical and financial challenges. So what would it take to accelerate the transition? And can the UN climate process still deliver meaningful results?

The hosts of next year's COP, Ethiopia, found a nationally tailored sporting metaphor to sum up the difficult, ongoing, sometimes tortuous process that the global environmental meetings can prove to be.

Speaking at the end of Friday's plenary to confirm Addis Ababa's intentions to host next year's summit, Gedion Timothewos, the foreign minister and upcoming president of COP32, made reference to Ethiopia's longstanding success in endurance running in track and field.

Referring to the COP process as a marathon, not a sprint, Timothewos said the country was looking forward to picking up the baton from Turkey and leading the next leg of the relay, taking the ongoing process forward into 2027.

His comments came during a late-night plenary session, but more talks are still expected in Antalya.

Shortly after 9 p.m. local time in Antalya, delegates gathered for a plenary session, well past the official closing time.

The Turkish presidency's opening address was partly drowned out by a protester shouting objections in English such as "you are killing the future," and gaining applause within the chamber.

"We've been negotiating for days to find the common ground," COP31 President Murat Kurum, Turkey's environment minister, told the plenary. He hailed the progress made and said the world "waits for good news from this room."

But the plenary session noted how several of the more ambitious aspects of a political declaration remained incomplete, with no agreement yet reached at the summit.
