The UN's IPCC should have already started work on its next series of multi-year scientific assessments on climate change's progress, but the process is on hold at the urging of countries including fossil fuel-producing giants Russia, Saudi Arabia and Iran.

Veteran climate justice advocate Victor Menotti of the Demand Climate Justice NGO told DW that European governments appeared to be copying US President Donald Trump's negotiating playbook at COP this year.

"We know the US isn't here, but we can see that Germany and other European governments have learned a few of the Trump tactics, maybe in the trade disputes with the US," Menotti said. "And we see them applying here in Antalya, where it's like: Don't admit to anything. Try to dilute your responsibilities. Weaken whatever obligations you have. And then take even more."

He argued these countries were also expecting unreasonable or unfeasible commitments from developing countries, for instance restrictions on what they can spend money on and obligations to report on their expenditures.

"Germany is subsidizing quite a bit of coal still and fossil fuels, LNG expansion infrastructure," he said, arguing that wealthier countries' energy spending also had room for improvement.

"We need the North to act first," Menotti said. "The deal from Paris, the deal from Rio 30 years ago, is that the North will lead. But yet we see too much bullying of the global South and not enough leadership like they agreed to."

#156. ‘Music was our teacher’: the sound system pioneers reviving the scene in Manchester and beyond

Manchester-based Megatone Sound Foundation and Baron Turbo Charge have rejuvenated the culture to offer a sense of belonging and empowerment

For a generation of young Black Mancunians, some of the history lessons that mattered most were not taught in classrooms, but through towering stacks of amps and speakers, turntables and Jamaican records.

“We didn’t know where we fit in. We didn’t know our history. Our parents were busy working two or three jobs,” says Errol “Barzy” Beaumont, a founder member of Baron Turbo Charge, Manchester’s long-running reggae sound system. “It was the records – Burning Spear, Bob Marley, Culture, that were telling us about our culture, which wasn’t on the school curriculum. For us, the sound system was not just entertainment – it was an education.”

More than 50 years since the sons and daughters of Windrush-generation pioneers adapted their parents’ music, DJing culture and technology to forge a Black British youth movement, Manchester’s sound system scene is enjoying a revival.

Keithly Brandy, an archivist of Manchester’s postwar Black history, has been working with museums and universities to preserve the flyers and paperwork that documented the scene in the 1970s and 80s and Winston Jones, the founder of Trafford Bar Studios, has been producing a podcast series about it.

It has come full circle. Manchester once looked to Jamaica for inspiration, but in recent years the Megatone Sound Foundation has set up and supported sound systems across the Black diaspora, from Kingston to the Gambia and Ethiopia, and Baron Turbo Charge are keeping reggae alive in the heart of Moss Side.

And, just as sound systems provided a lifeline for young Black people with few places to express themselves in postwar Britain, half a century later they are once again offering a sense of belonging and community to those same people, now in their 60s.

For Marcus, of Megatone Sound Foundation, the journey began in his parents’ front room in Old Trafford in 1976, when he was nine years old. Without his mother’s knowledge, he dismantled the family’s wooden stereogram, removing the turntable, speakers and valve amplifier before reassembling them in homemade cabinets upstairs.

“I started to build a little system called Conqueror. It was just like a house thing,” he says. “And then from there, it grew.”

Back then the emergent scene was concentrated in the house parties, community functions and youth clubs of Manchester neighbourhoods such as Hulme, Moss Side and Old Trafford.

The records varied depending on the venue and the company. Bob and Marcia’s anthem, Young, Gifted and Black, was a staple record parents approved of. But, away from the more conservative older generation, youth clubs such as Aquarius, Proctor’s, Birley and the Hideaway rocked to the sounds of Dillinger and Johnny Clarke. In what could be a male-dominated scene, 1980s sounds such as Kilowatt drew a female following with lovers rock.

No one could have imagined that ephemera from Manchester’s scene – such as a contract from the broadcaster David Rodigan’s appearance at the PSV club, the reggae venue founded by the city’s Black bus drivers – would one day be displayed in a museum. That contract – which features in the V&A East’s The Music is Black: A British Story exhibition in London – is among items preserved for decades by Brandy.

Back in 1976, Brandy set up his own sound system, Tyban Sounds, with two friends, “Big” Trevor Bell and “Young” Trevor Bell, inspired by the older generation of Manchester soundmen, including Lord Cass, Bugga Levi, Count Leroy, Count Sam, Rocket and Angel Hi-Fi, who had brought the culture with them from the Caribbean. “You would see these people at weddings and christenings – and be in awe of them,” Brandy says.

Brandy still recalls the excitement of bringing London’s Conqueror sound system to Manchester Carnival and the after-party at Abasindi’s women’s co-operative – having persuaded his mother, the carnival pioneer Locita Brandy, to give the young people a chance.

The sound system – a competitive collective of DJs, MCs, sound engineers, crew and their mobile, custom-built, bass-heavy audio set-up – was invented in 1940s Jamaica, with the original sounds playing American R&B and mento music in Kingston.

In the US, the culture would shape hip-hop, after the Kingston-born DJ Kool Herc set up a sound system in the Bronx, while in the UK, sound system culture evolved through time, bringing everything from roots reggae and lovers rock to house, garage and jungle records to the mainstream.

Despite that winding journey – shaping artists including Soul II Soul, Maxi Priest and Massive Attack along the way – reggae is the essence of the culture.

“Back then the music was like a newspaper,” Jones says of 70s reggae. “It was teaching us about history, it was teaching us about Marcus Garvey, about Nelson Mandela. It was teaching us that Africa was our home – the music was our teacher.”

At the heart of Trafford Bar Studios, the creative space Jones founded and funded with compensation from his experiences as part of the Windrush scandal, young people can learn skills such as DJing, photography, videography and podcasting without charge. It echoes the ethos of the sound system movement: collective and individual empowerment through music and creativity.

Starting with the Guardian’s own history, Guardian journalists explore the legacies of enslavement and reparative justice around the world

“It’s about resilience,” Jones says. “A kid leaves school and believes he can’t do anything, but we give you hope when you come in here.”

Jones funded the Pioneers of Manchester’s Soundsystem era podcast to ensure voices from a significant chapter in Manchester’s Black cultural history are preserved for posterity. “All these soundmen, everybody have a story to tell,” he said.

The Baron Turbo Charge founder Errol Beaumont set out into the scene with his friends Junior Wilson and the late Melvyn “Wizzydan” Joseph as teenagers in the mid-70s, when Black people faced an unofficial societal colour bar.

The trio saved £10 each to buy a secondhand valve guitar amplifier to add to the records, speakers and old turntable they already had.

On the day of the dance, realising their speakers had no cabinets, they nailed together boxes using timber from a building site and hauled the gear up the staircase of flats on Bonsall Street, on the edge of Manchester’s long-demolished, brutalist Hulme Crescents estate.

“We hadn’t tested the system or anything. The party was on the fourth floor, we built it on the first, and by the time we got to the second floor, it fell apart,” Beaumont laughs. From that moment on, the boys decided to “get serious”.

Awareness that his generation was facing a different kind of exclusion motivated Beaumont, now 67, to return to playing dances after a decades-long hiatus.

“There’s nothing really for my generation now, as entertainment,” he says. “That was one of the reasons we brought the sound back out – because there’s a lot of people like ourselves who still want to put on their nice clothes and go out, just like we did back in the day.”

This makes the West Indian Sports and Social Centre – the historic Moss Side community hub – a key venue, one of the few places where large sound systems can still recreate the old atmosphere.

Meanwhile, as well as establishing sounds in Africa and the Caribbean, fundraising for charitable causes, and organising events celebrating community elders, the Megatone Sound Foundation is feted by audiences in France, Greece, Hungary and Poland.

“What makes Manchester sounds special is that Manchester people have this warm demeanour, and a multicultural attitude to things,” Jones says. “If you go back to the days of [Moss Side nightclubs] the Nile and the Reno, white people, Black people, everybody was rubbing shoulders. It’s a working-class attitude, it’s a solidarity.”

#154. Reform UK considers pact with Restore Britain to overcome stalling performance in polls

Thursday’s byelection in Castle Bromwich will be latest test for Nigel Farage’s party as rival eats into support

Voters go to the polls in the village of Castle Bromwich in the West Midlands on Thursday in a little-noticed election that is the latest test for Reform at a time when it is particularly vulnerable.

Yet, while it is the Conservatives who are edging ahead in recent polls, the decisive difference could well be the performance of Restore Britain – the hard-right party set up by Rupert Lowe after the MP fell out with Nigel Farage.

Reform has lost no less than five elections since September, on one occasion by as little as two votes, after Restore attracted voters who might otherwise have voted for Farage’s party.

The possibility of offering an electoral pact to Restore is now under discussion among some figures at Reform’s Millbank HQ, the Guardian understands. There is also fervent internal opposition from some who balk at the ethno-nationalism that has found a home in Restore.

“Just take a look at some of the people who have attached themselves to Restore, as well as what some of their people have said about Jews and Muslims in their comments,” said one.

At the same time, and even after an aborted attempt to bury the hatchet between Lowe and Farage, others in Reform are leaving the door open to a reconciliation with Restore at a time when the party needs to find a way to overcome its stalling support.

“There’s an argument that we should be going after Restore and we’ll benefit from that, but there is also one where we work with them … and, to be honest, I don’t know the answer yet,” said one.

One senior Reform source said the idea of a “non-aggression pact” with Restore was in the air and there was a “deal to be done” but was more sceptical about a deeper tie up or merger.

At the same time, other senior Reform figures who are close to Farage are pushing for him to go much further towards a detente with Rupert Lowe, with efforts under way to push them back together.

Among Reform insiders, there is an acknowledgment that Farage is at least as exercised and frustrated about Restore eating into the party’s vote as he is about the investigations into his own financial affairs. One Reform politician said they envisaged Lowe ultimately being readmitted, but that the price would be the exit of Zia Yusuf, who had accused Lowe of assaulting him amid the furore that led to the MP’s suspension from the party.

However, that was before Yusuf this week appeared to praise Lowe in a conversation with the Spectator’s editor, Michael Gove. “Rupert is a force of nature,” said Yusuf, who went on to describe him as a “formidable person” in comments that drew a strikingly warm response from Lowe himself, who tweeted that he appreciated Yusuf’s acknowledgment of Restore’s progress.

“I suspect on policy and trust in politics, Yusuf and I have more in common than the former Tory ministers in Reform’s leadership,” he added.

Last week, there was a public display of Reform working with Restore when the former Tory MPs Andrew Rosindell and Suella Braverman signed an early day motion by Lowe opposing a Liberal Democrat bid to pave the way for census questions to include more ethnicity options to allow people to define themselves as Black English or Asian English.

Even within Restore, and away from the public rhetoric, there is curiosity about the openness of Reform to some form of pact as the speculation about an early general election persists.

“Our offer remains on the table and we are open to negotiating that offer,” said a Restore source, who cited events in the summer when it looked like a reconciliation between Farage and Lowe was in the offing.

“It is Farage and others who have outright rejected it, but the ball is in their court.”

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

On that occasion, what seemed like tentative moves towards peace broke down amid a fresh row between the two leaders, with Lowe accusing Farage of lying after the latter had said he had reached out but had not heard back.

What remains clear now though is that Restore’s consistent nudging of 5% in serious national polls has taken some in its own ranks and even in Reform – whose leader predicted that its rival “would be lucky to get 1% anywhere” – by surprise. A Restore source said of Reform: “It feels like they’re having a bit of an identity crisis and falling below 20% has clearly given them pause for thought.”

More stark has been the proliferation of Restore branches across the UK – sometimes numbering just a handful of people but in others much larger – and the steady dripfeed of defections from Reform.

Last week, those who joined Lowe at a lavish central London dinner for members of Restore’s “Cromwell Club” for those donating £2,500 a year included Holly Vallance, the estranged wife of Reform’s honorary treasurer Nick Candy, who had frequently been cited as a potential candidate for Farage’s party.

Also there was Charlton Edwards, who was Reform’s treasurer until recently and a figure at the very heart of the courting of donors by Farage’s party.

Aside from the beef between Farage and Lowe – arguably the biggest alpha males on the right – the biggest stumbling block to a pact is the antisemitism within Restore, said one source in Reform, which has made a concerted effort to court the British Jewish community.

“Restore emerged because Rupert was out for revenge and it’s hard to ignore. But there are also things that have been said by people around Restore about Jews, Muslims, that we find deeply uncomfortable,” they said, citing the explicit antisemitism directed towards Restore’s Molly Kingsley online when Lowe unveiled her as his party’s “accountability” spokesperson.

Figures such as Charlie Downes, a Restore election candidate who is often seen by Lowe’s side, have said that only white Christians can truly be British. Another Restore member who was pictured alongside Lowe at last week’s Cromwell Club dinner, the former GB News presenter Calvin Robinson, has been condemned in recent days for comments in which he talked of the need to “drive out the Jews” from the US.

However, extreme language can also be found in Reform. Last week, the Welsh Senedd member Joe Martin became the first frontline Reform politician to use the term “remigration” – one that originated on the far right and is frequently used by Restore.

Others in Reform, meanwhile, are impressed by the social media strategy of Restore, which has on occasion been backed by Elon Musk.

#153. Germany's religious freedom report: Authoritarian regimes, holy wars, and spies abroad

Harassment, persecution, surveillance: Across much of the world, people of faith are facing growing pressure. A new German government report is unusually direct in criticizing China in particular.

The report spans more than 150 pages, but its conclusion can be summed up in a single sentence: "The global situation regarding freedom of religion and belief deteriorated during the reporting period," said Thomas Rachel, the German government's commissioner for freedom of religion and belief, and a member of parliament for the center-right Christian Democratic Union (CDU). Recently adopted by the Cabinet, the government's fourth report on the subject covers developments from 2023 to 2025.

The government has issued a dedicated report on freedom of religion and belief around the world. According to the report, this freedom is facing "increasing pressure" globally. The focus is not primarily on the suppression of religious institutions or structures, but on the situation of individuals who hold a religious belief, or none at all.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The importance of religious freedom is reflected in the way the government commissioner describes the issue: "Religion and faith are of great importance to four out of five people around the world and are central to their identity. People wake up with God and go to sleep with God. It is part of their everyday lives." Research by the Pew Research Center suggests that about 84% of the world's population identifies with a certain religion.

At a time when authoritarian regimes are gaining ground and expanding their surveillance capabilities, the report paints a bleak picture. The growing number of violent conflicts, the rise of authoritarian rule, and the spread of populism have led to "painful restrictions on freedom of religion and belief around the world."

The German government report does not attempt to provide a statistical tally of attacks or the number of people facing persecution. Instead, it examines 30 countries as case studies where, in the federal government's view, freedom of religion and belief is being restricted. These include about six countries each from Africa, Asia, and the Arab world. In Europe, the report focuses on Belarus, Russia, Ukraine, and Turkey, while in Latin America it highlights Mexico, Nicaragua, and Cuba. It also identifies shortcomings in five of the world's six most populous countries: China, India, Indonesia, Pakistan, and Nigeria.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Regarding India, the report refers to "Hindu nationalist efforts" that threaten the equal treatment of all officially recognized religions. It states that the generally peaceful coexistence of different faiths is "repeatedly overshadowed by tensions and, at times, violence." Both Muslim and Christian communities report growing intolerance and hate speech. According to the report, violent Hindu extremist groups have carried out a "large number of physical attacks" against Muslims, while the police and government authorities often respond inadequately.

In China, the German government identifies a "significant tightening of the legal framework of state control at the expense of freedom of religion and belief." According to the report, this particularly affects Uyghurs, Tibetans and churches that lack official state approval and are subject to harsh measures by the authorities. The law on the "Promotion of Ethnic Unity and Progress," which came into force on July 1, 2026, subordinates religion to "the formation of national identity" and patriotic education.

One of the countries where the situation has deteriorated most dramatically is Nicaragua, which has severely restricted freedom of religion and belief since 2018. The repression has primarily targeted the Catholic Church. According to the report, "intimidation, persecution, imprisonment, and expulsion" have forced around 130 priests and nuns to leave the country.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The reports reserves some of its sharpest criticism for Kirill, the patriarch of the Russian Orthodox Church. Under his leadership, the church has strongly supported Russia's war against Ukraine and portrayed it as a "holy war." According to the report, "weapons are blessed, Russian soldiers are absolved of their sins, and donations are collected for the war." It is perhaps the clearest example in the report of how religion can be used to justify oppression and violence.

The report explicitly addresses the threat posed by "transnational repression" (TNR), which it describes as a "growing global problem" that also affects Germany. The term refers to the persecution or intimidation of individuals by their country of origin beyond its own borders.

Ultimately, it argues, this amounts to a "continuation of domestic repression." Such "cross-border repression" has become considerably easier through the growing availability of digital means of repression, including smear campaigns on social media and spyware.

These forms of pressure range from physical attacks on individuals to digital assaults such as online intimidation and defamation. In some cases, repression is also carried out by threatening family members who remain in the country of origin. Russia and Iran, for example, are identified as the "main perpetrators of transnational repression" in Germany.

China is a particularly prominent example of religious and ideological tensions. Repression by Beijing in Germany primarily targets members of groups that the country's Communist Party classifies as "hostile to the system." These include Uyghurs, Tibetans, and members of the Falun Gong movement. According to the report, they are "also subject to surveillance and repressive measures in Germany," while relatives living in China are pressured.

Thomas Rachel hopes the newly published report will be "discussed in as many Bundestag committees as possible" and that members of parliament will "engage with the issue accordingly." Previous government reports on the subject have been debated in the Bundestag plenary at least once and, in some cases, twice.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The issue's place within the German government has also changed during the current legislative term. Commissioner Thomas Rachel is now based at the Foreign Ministry, while both of his predecessors since 2018 were attached to the Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development. Today, Rachel says, the importance of religion is being "taken into account" in German foreign policy. He also points to his involvement in the Foreign Ministry's diplomatic training programs.

In doing so, the government led by Chancellor Friedrich Merz (CDU) reversed a structural decision made by the previous center-left coalition, which was led from 2021 to 2025 by Chancellor Olaf Scholz of the Social Democratic Party (SPD). The Scholz government had reduced the issue's prominence by assigning it to the "Religion and Foreign Policy" division. Religion and foreign policy have now regained a more prominent place within government.

One example is the current foreign minister, Johann Wadephul, who has already visited the Vatican more often than his two predecessors combined. In addition, alongside Rachel, the ministry has for some time maintained a dedicated unit headed by an ambassador that deals with, among other things, "fundamental issues relating to religion and foreign policy."

#151. Princess of Wales speaks about impact of cancer diagnosis in BBC film Heal

Documentary records Catherine taking on Three Peaks challenge and finding ‘sense of hope alongside uncertainty’

The Princess of Wales has spoken candidly about the impact of her cancer diagnosis in a BBC documentary to be aired later this month.

“Cancer does change you,” Catherine, 44, says in the trailer for the 30-minute programme. “It changes your body. It changes you physically, but there’s a much, much deeper part to what a cancer diagnosis shifts in you.”

The film records Catherine taking on the Three Peaks challenge – climbing the three highest mountains in England, Scotland and Wales in 24 hours – and meeting with other people undergoing cancer treatment. Described by the BBC as a “highly personal film”, the documentary, titled Heal, will be shown on BBC One on 29 November.

The trailer emerged as it was reported that King Charles will voluntarily pay “mansion tax” on relevant royal household properties, despite crown exemption. The high-value council tax surcharge, often referred to as a mansion tax, will take effect in 2028 and apply to properties valued at more than £2m.

Catherine was diagnosed with an undisclosed form of cancer in early 2024, for which she received chemotherapy, and by January 2025 announced she was in remission.

She was treated at the Royal Marsden hospital in London, a world-leading oncology centre, with funds from the distribution of the documentary, which will also be broadcast in other countries, going to the Royal Marsden cancer charity.

When people successfully finish cancer treatment at the hospital they often ring a bell in celebration. “I didn’t ring the bell,” Catherine says in the trailer, speaking to her brother, James Middleton, as they are walking together.

“Maybe this is your ringing the bell,” he says, in reference to her undertaking the Three Peaks challenge and making the programme.

When she completed the challenge in June, 18 months after announcing that the cancer was in remission, she became the first royal to have achieved the feat – climbing Ben Nevis, Scafell Pike and Yr Wyddfa in less than 24 hours, having started on a Saturday evening.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

In the programme, which will be introduced by the presenter Kirsty Young, Catherine talks about how she has learned through her recovery to find “a sense of hope and joy alongside uncertainty”.

Kate Phillips, the BBC’s chief content officer, said she hoped the documentary “will resonate with BBC viewers and provide comfort and support to those navigating their own health challenges”.

Heal will be shown on BBC One at 18:40 GMT on 29 November and will be available on iPlayer. It will air internationally on NBCUniversal in the US on 30 November; Channel 9 in Australia on 1 December; and CTV and Noovo in Canada on 7 December.

#150. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#147. Cold gives way to rain in parts of UK as weather warning issued for south-west England

Flood alerts in place and risk of power cuts as more than 100mm predicted to fall on Dartmoor on Sunday

Cold snaps are to make way for heavy rain in some parts of the country as the Met Office issued a weather warning for south-west England.

It said there will be a slow-moving band of rain moving across Devon and Cornwall, with more than 100mm due to fall on Dartmoor. The warning will be in place from 6am on Sunday until midnight.

The Met Office said there was a risk of power cuts and added that driving conditions could be difficult. There are active flood alerts in the area.

Cold weather health warnings are in place in northern England until Tuesday morning. The lowest temperature this autumn so far was recored on Friday night.

North-west and north-east England, and Yorkshire and the Humber, have had yellow alerts issued by the UK Health Security Agency. It suggests there is a risk to vulnerable people and that conditions may put pressure on the health service and people should take care when travelling.

Friday night had the lowest temperature since early January, as Tulloch Bridge in the Highlands recorded -10.6C. It beat this autumn’s record of -8.4C at the same weather station on Thursday.

The Met Office said there could be a widespread frost across the UK on Saturday night, but there will be warmer and largely drier conditions outside south-west England on Sunday.

The Met Office chief forecaster, Matthew Lehnert, said: “Within the warning area, 20-40mm of rain is likely quite widely, but some parts of south Devon and Cornwall could see 60-80mm.

“Over Dartmoor, there is a chance of more than 100mm falling through the day. This slow-moving band of rain will gradually ease on Sunday night, but flooding and travel disruption is possible within the warning area, with south Devon and south Cornwall more likely to see higher totals.

“There is still some uncertainty about where the heaviest rain will fall, so it’s important to stay up to date with the forecast and any warning updates.”

#144. Suffolk village votes to ‘leave the UK’ over plans to house asylum seekers nearby

Local MP says legitimate criticisms of asylum housing plans are being co-opted by opposition parties ‘stirring up racial discord’

Residents of a Suffolk village have voted to “leave the UK” in a referendum held in response to government plans to house 930 asylum seekers at a nearby former military base.

Approximately 500 people in Barnham were eligible to take part in the symbolic vote on Saturday. There were 257 votes for independence, 16 against and one spoiled ballot.

After the vote, Helen Beck, the chair of Barnham Residents’ Association, said Barnham’s flag would be a stone curlew on a “Suffolk blue background”.

RAF Barnham was one of three Ministry of Defence sites named in June by the government as potential locations for new developments to house asylum seekers.

A similar referendum was held in Piddington, Oxfordshire in September, with residents also voting to leave the UK amid plans to house more than 1,000 migrants at another former military base.

In Barnham, the plans have prompted protests outside the site. The referendum asked residents whether they supported seeking a “crown dependency status”, which is a similar system to that of Jersey, Guernsey and the Isle of Man.

John Bauer, the parish council chair, said this would allow the village to “retain loyalty to the crown” but remove itself from Home Office jurisdiction.

While in opposition the Labour party had attacked the Conservative policy to house asylum seekers on military bases; once in government, however, the party backtracked. The Conservatives have also reversed their position, with Kemi Badenoch telling frustrated villagers “the Conservative party is on your side”.

“We know that the problem of the boats needs to stop and we have got a plan to stop it,” she said on Friday.

Earlier this month, it was announced construction firm Kier would design the new camp at RAF Barnham.

Before Saturday’s vote Labour’s Dr Peter Prinsley, the local MP, said he was against the development of the site. Prinsley said the plans resembled a “concentration camp”, and warned that planning permission may be difficult to attain due to environmental concerns about rare species living in the area – in particular, stone curlews – with previous development applications nearby having been turned down.

“This is a symbolic referendum,” Prinsley said. “It’s not going to produce a binding result. I’m expecting that [the result] will be hugely in favour of becoming a crown dependency. There are 500 electors, so I’m expecting it to be 500 – nil.”

He added that the government “is doing an awful lot to deal with the irregular migration problem”, and highlighted that net migration may come down to “more or less zero by the end of the year”.

But he added that he didn’t think “the large army camps in remote locations are ever likely to be appropriate for the distribution of people seeking asylum. The fact is that people who arrive here on small boats are not illegal migrants. They are people who are seeking asylum. Our political opponents have described these people as ‘illegals, criminals, rapists’, and all sorts of things that they’re not. They’re just people who have arrived on small boats. The government is getting a grip on this problem, but it’s a complicated problem.”

He said the proposed Barnham site had particular issues which may make it unsuitable. “It’s a remote location without any pedestrian access, it’s on a very hazardous main road. When it was used as military accommodation there were serious accidents on that road.

“It’s also a site of special scientific interest because it is the nesting site for stone curlews, which will make any planning application very difficult – even a government planning application – because these are worldwide protections.”

He suggested “disused office blocks in some of the larger cities” may be more appropriate places to house asylum seekers, with more local services available.

“It feels like a concentration camp,” Prinsley added. “It doesn’t feel like a place that has a compassionate approach to people who are fleeing persecution and are being processed for asylum.”

He also said legitimate criticism of the plans had been co-opted by opposition parties who are aiming to “stir up racial discord”.

A Home Office spokesperson said: “We recognise the strength of feeling in local communities and are listening carefully to the concerns being raised.

“The government is working on a fairer distribution of asylum accommodation across the country, with over £1bn saved in costs since the election.

“This includes closing all asylum hotels and moving to alternative accommodation, including former military sites. These are designed to be self-contained, with 24/7 security and CCTV, to reduce the impact on local communities.”

#142. Myanmar committed genocide against Rohingya minority, world’s top court rules

Judgment finds ‘intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population’ after case seen as critical test for dealing with most serious of state crimes

Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority during a 2017 military campaign, the world’s highest court has ruled, in a historic and unprecedented decision.

The judgment from the international court of justice (ICJ) – which has never previously found a state responsible for genocide – ends a seven-year legal battle that was seen as a critical global test for dealing with the most serious of all state crimes.

The ICJ president, Yuji Iwasawa, said a majority of nine judges on the 15-seat court found that “the Republic of the Union of Myanmar has committed genocide against the Rohingya”.

The 2016-17 campaign by Myanmar’s military forced more than 700,000 mostly Muslim Rohingya to flee across the border into Bangladesh, arriving with reports of mass killings of adults and children, gang-rapes and burned-down villages.

Nearly a decade later, in a refugee camp in Bangladesh, crowds of Rohingya people watching on outdoor TV screens leaped up in celebration and applause as the final verdict was read out.

One Rohingya survivor, Yousuf Ali, had travelled to the court to hear the verdict. “Today, we have finally been heard, and it is a profound relief,” he said. “This judgment gives hope to our children and the next generation of Rohingya. But our fight is not over.”

Governments had been anxiously awaiting the decision handed down in the Netherlands, where the UN-run court is based, in part because the ruling will set a precedent for genocide claims, notably a high-profile case against Israel.

In what could become a crucial factor for the Gaza case, where Israel claims its mass killings are part of a counter-terrorism operation, judges dismissed Myanmar’s claims of carrying out legitimate military operations against insurgents.

Genocide, defined after the Nazi Holocaust by a 1948 UN convention, is harder to prove than war crimes because it requires evidence of specific “intent” to destroy a national, ethnic, racial or religious group. Iwasawa said during the reading of the decision that the court was “fully convinced that Myanmar committed acts falling under … the genocide convention with an intent to destroy a substantial part of the Rohingya population in Myanmar”.

After the ruling, Britain’s foreign minister, Ed Miliband, said the government was expelling Myanmar’s defence attaché and called “urgently” for a session at the UN security council, the body that has the power to enforce ICJ rulings.

The case was raised by the Gambia, a Muslim-majority state, on behalf of Myanmar survivors. It rested on claims of genocidal statements and “patterns of conduct” that showed attempts to destroy the group during assaults on Rakhine state, including gunning down people as they fled and blocking their return.

In January, the ICJ held closed-door sessions to hear from survivors who had travelled from camps in Bangladesh. More than a million Rohingya still live there, in the world’s largest refugee settlement, and fewer than 600,000 are estimated to remain in Buddhist-majority Myanmar.

Refugees who had fled to Bangladesh told the Guardian in 2017 of a massacre in the village of Tula Toli, where survivors described systematic shooting of civilians, mass graves and the drowning of children in a river. That village, which satellite footage later showed had been burned down, was one of several analysed by rights investigators and built a case for genocide.

During hearings, the Gambian justice minister, Dawda Jallow, told the court the case was not “about esoteric issues of international law”. “It is about real people, real stories and a real group of human beings,” said Jallow. “The Rohingya of Myanmar. They have been targeted for destruction.”

Legal experts see Thursday’s judgment as a key test for sexual violence in genocide assessments, as the convention says the crime can include acts with the intent to “prevent births”. Investigators have said gang-rapes and soldiers’ mutilation of women’s genitals were carried out intentionally and systematically to stop reproduction.

The court appeared to back that assessment, accusing the military of using sexual violence to “physically and mentally impair the willingness and ability of the [Rohingya] population to procreate”, referring to cultural stigma for rape survivors. Myanmar had also imposed other measures to restrict births, the court ruled, such as limits of two children per Rohingya family.

Myanmar has denied carrying out a genocide, claiming its actions targeted a Rohingya militant group that conducted guerrilla-style attacks. Its military responded with massive “clearance operations”.

However, judges rejected Myanmar’s claim that its actions were primarily intended as counter-terrorism operations, or even that they were an attempt to punish or forcibly displace the population but without committing genocide.

The country’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-17 attacks, has a pending arrest warrant from the international criminal court (ICC), which, unlike the ICJ, prosecutes individuals and not states, on allegations of crimes against humanity.

The former general overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi in a 2021 coup, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely discredited.

While the ICJ does not have the power to enforce rulings, its decisions are considered binding, carry diplomatic weight and can shape international action, including sanctions.

During a reading of the decision that lasted more than two hours in a packed Great Hall of Justice in The Hague, Iwasawa called for Myanmar to provide reparations and restitution. This included allowing the safe return of Rohingya to their homes, returning or replacing their property, and providing physical and mental rehabilitation.

The ruling was also significant as it formally classified the Rohingya as a distinct ethnic group under international law, something they have been denied by Myanmar, which refers to them as Bengalis or foreign migrants.

Reached by phone shortly after he left the courtroom, the ​lead counsel for the case against Myanmar told the Guardian the mood had been emotional in the room as the judgment was read out in front of his legal team, which included Rohingya.

“This is the ICJ recognising their history of discrimination, recognising them as a group, recognising the horrific violence they endured and suffered,” said Arsalan Suleman. “It was a really emotional moment for them and the whole team. We felt the weight of the court’s judgment.”

Israel and its chief ally, the US, have been closely following the Myanmar case, as the decision provides the strongest hint of how judges could assess the Gaza case, brought by South Africa. UN independent investigations, as well as genocide scholars, have already said Israel has committed acts of genocide in Gaza.

A judgment finding Myanmar had breached the convention has been predicted to lead to the US imposing sanctions on ICJ judges, including this year’s Nobel peace prize winner, Navi Pillay.

Washington had considered the Rohingya crisis to be a genocide, based on a decision by the Biden administration. However, the administration of Donald Trump has been on the offensive against international rights bodies, including imposing sanctions on the ICC.

The US state department said in a statement that it would study the ICJ’s decision “to fully assess its implications” and that “the proper interpretation of the genocide convention is of paramount importance, as is preventing politicisation of the ICJ”.

It reiterated US support for the people of Myanmar and said it shared “grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups”.

The Trump administration of has been implacably opposed to international legal organisations. On Thursday, the president used a trilateral meeting with Russia and China on Thursday to condemn “rogue globalist organisations”. But Trump’s ire has been directed most towards the ICC, a separate body which the administration has vowed to dismantle “brick by brick”, rather than the ICJ.

Two other ICJ cases dealing with atrocities in the 1990s Balkan wars fell short of finding states directly responsible for genocide, although Serbia was found legally responsible for failing to prevent or punish genocide committed by Bosnian Serb troops at the Srebrenica massacre.

Other UN tribunals have established genocide in only a handful of cases since the Holocaust, including Cambodia’s Khmer Rouge rule in the 1970s and the 1994 mass killings in Rwanda.

#138. ‘Everyone struggled for this justice’: tears in court as Rohingya vindicated in Myanmar genocide ruling

Relief in the immediate aftermath of a landmark ruling, but what happens next is in the hands of governments around the world

There were tears in the halls of The Hague in the Netherlands and an eruption of applause in the refugee camps of Bangladesh as the scattered communities of Rohingya watched the UN’s top court rule the Myanmar military had committed genocide against them.

For almost a decade, the Rohingya have awaited this moment; recognition from the international court of justice (ICJ) that Myanmar in 2017 intentionally tried to destroy the mostly Muslim minority group through a campaign of mass killings and rape. More than 10,000 Rohingya were killed and 700,000 fled to neighbouring Bangladesh.

By the time they spilled into the court’s halls, the relief was evident. A Rohingya community leader threw a big thumbs up as he left the courtroom and others immediately took out their phones to call family in the refugee camps.

Salma, 30, a Rohingya survivor who lost family members in the 2017 genocide, said: “Everyone worked hard and struggled for this justice, for this cause. When they hear it today, everyone will be happy, thank God. I don’t know how to express it or even what to do with myself.”

Another Rohingya present, who cannot be named because of safety concerns, said they cried as the judge went through each of the rulings.

“During the last few minutes I couldn’t even sit down any more, I had to move to the back because I didn’t know how I’d react,” they said. “I saw some of the other Rohingya around me crying. They were so emotional.

“Now the responsibility is with the international governments. It has been declared genocide now and they have to act.”

In Bangladesh, where most of the Rohingya population have lived – in the world’s largest refugee camp – since being forced to flee in 2017, the verdict was translated into the Rohingya language for the crowds who had gathered to watch on screens specially set up for the purpose.

They immediately leapt up, cheering, applauding and shouting gratitude for the Gambia – the faraway African nation that brought the case to the ICJ on behalf of the stateless minority. Many held posters that read “Thank You Gambia” and “Justice = Safe Return”.

“Since we arrived here, we have been waiting and looking forward to this justice,” said Farida Khatun, 50, who has been a refugee in Bangladesh since 2017, when her village, Tula Toli, was targeted by soldiers. The village in north-western Myanmar, near the Bangladesh border, was the scene of a massacre in August of that year, with reports of mass killings and gang rapes.

She said, however, that she does not expect to be able to return home. Tula Toli was completely destroyed by Myanmar’s forces and no longer exists. “Even though today’s verdict makes us happy, my heart still cannot believe that they will take us back in true peace.”

The case has taken seven years to reach this point, with Rohingya researchers working to collect testimony from survivors. The initial hope for justice had faded during the long wait for a ruling. Many were also left deflated during early hearings when the one-time pro-democracy hero Aung San Suu Kyi defended the Myanmar military at The Hague.

Before today’s ruling, some were optimistic, others were prepared to be disappointed, but all said they knew themselves that they were victims of genocide.

The tension was finally released when the court declared Myanmar was responsible for genocide against the Rohingya, highlighting the use of sexual violence and attacks on children alongside the killing of Rohingya who tried to flee violence, which it said meant the violence amounted to more than ethnic cleansing.

The court ordered Myanmar to provide reparations for the Rohingya and end continuing breaches of the genocide convention.

There is now also an international criminal court investigation into the violence of 2017, looking specifically at the crime of mass deportation, and for which prosecutors in 2024 sought an arrest warrant for Myanmar’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing.

With continuing violence against the Rohingya, the possibility of a return to Myanmar, as happened after previous exoduses to Bangladesh in 1978 and the early 1990s, remains unlikely.

But many hope the ICJ ruling will be the start of a process of ensuring justice and lead to real international pressure to ensure their safety in Myanmar.

“We will be able to return to our homeland – with our rights, with our citizenship, and having received our full security. We will be able to return,” says Salma.

#135. US reacts cautiously to Myanmar genocide ruling as EU praises ‘critical’ role of UN’s top court

Reactions to ICJ ruling underline global divisions over court with China raising concerns about judicial overreach

The US has greeted with caution the world’s highest court’s ruling that Myanmar committed genocide against its Rohingya minority, while China has raised concerns of judicial overreach.

Reactions to Thursday’s historic ruling underlined deep international division over the role of the court and similar institutions. The international court of justice (ICJ) had never previously found a state responsible for genocide and the decision could set a precedent for future genocide claims, notably a case against Israel.

The EU hailed a “landmark” judgment from the ICJ, while the UK reaffirmed “full support” for the court and the international legal order. Australia called on Myanmar to comply fully with the judgment and its obligations under international law.

But China, which has offered rare international support for Myanmar’s military rulers since they seized power five years ago, highlighted “issues such as jurisdiction overreach and expanded interpretation”.

“China … believes that such practices are not conducive to the settlement of disputes,” the foreign ministry spokesperson, Guo Jiakun, told a news briefing when asked about the ICJ ruling. “The international community should make joint efforts in this direction to avoid complicating this issue.”

Western nations cut ties with the unstable and poor south-east Asian country after the 2021 coup that ousted a civilian-led government.

Myanmar’s presidential spokesperson said on Friday that the ruling was “unfair”, noting the judges’ nine to six vote. The country’s military rulers have denied carrying out a genocide, claiming their actions targeted a Rohingya militant group that conducted insurgent attacks.

“When we look at the result, not all 15 judges agreed with the decision,” said Khaing Khaing Soe, speaking at a press conference in the capital, Naypyidaw. “So this was not a unanimous decision … I would say it is not fair.”

The US state department said on Thursday that Washington was examining the judgment, while stressing the need to avoid “politicisation” of the ICJ.

Donald Trump’s administration has taken a dim view of international law, vowing to dismantle the international criminal court (ICC), but has yet to question the legitimacy of the ICJ.

The state department reiterated US support for the people of Myanmar and said it shared “grave concerns over the continued abuses of human rights against Rohingya Muslims and members of other minority groups”, noting that the US was the largest single humanitarian donor to the Rohingya humanitarian crisis response.

In Brussels, the EU said Myanmar must fully implement the ICJ’s ruling “without delay” and that “compliance with ICJ’s decisions is not optional, it is an obligation under international law”.

“The European Union holds the ICJ in the highest regard and remains steadfast in its support for its critical role in the peaceful resolution of disputes and the upholding of the rule of law at international level,” an EU spokesperson said in a statement.

“The Rohingya people have endured immense suffering, including displacement and violence, and their plight must not be forgotten. This landmark ruling underscores the imperative of justice for victims and the necessity of upholding international law.”

The UN secretary general, António Guterres, called the ruling “a demonstration of international justice and a reaffirmation that impunity for such horrific acts must not stand”.

The UK expelled Myanmar’s defence attache and called for measures to “ensure the court’s ruling sends a clear message that such crimes cannot be committed with impunity”.

The ruling caps a lengthy legal campaign since August 2017 when Myanmar’s military launched what it called a “clearance campaign” in Rakhine state in response to an attack by a Rohingya insurgent group. Reports rapidly emerged of mass rapes and killings by security forces, as well as the burning of thousands of homes.

Judges rejected Myanmar’s claim that its actions were primarily intended as counter-terrorism operations, or even that they were an attempt to punish or forcibly displace the population but without committing genocide.

The country’s leader, Min Aung Hlaing, who headed the military during the 2016-17 attacks, has a pending arrest warrant from the ICC – which, unlike the ICJ, prosecutes individuals and not states – on allegations of crimes against humanity.

The former general overthrew the elected government of Aung San Suu Kyi, triggering a civil war that has left tens of thousands dead. He was installed as civilian president in April after elections that were widely discredited.

While the ICJ does not have the power to enforce rulings, its decisions are considered binding, carry diplomatic weight and can shape international action, including sanctions.

Under the 1948 convention, to establish that genocide occurred the court had to find that there was an intent to destroy the Rohingya “in whole or in part”.

About 1.2 million Rohingya refugees now live in Bangladesh, primarily in crowded camps across the Cox’s Bazar district, where they endure harsh conditions marked by fire hazards, infectious diseases, monsoon landslides, inadequate sanitation and strict work restrictions.

Judges at the court based in The Hague have also been asked to rule on other allegations of genocide.

South Africa has accused Israel of breaching the genocide convention during its offensive in Gaza, a claim Israel vehemently denies. That case is years from completion. An interim ICJ judgment in 2024 ordered Israel to ensure its forces do not commit acts of genocide against Palestinians in Gaza.

Ukraine also has a case pending against Russia. Shortly after Moscow’s full-scale invasion in February 2022, Kyiv accused Russia of fabricating genocide claims against Ukraine to justify the attack.

#134. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#129. New EU budget proposal aims at tax gobal tech giants

European Council President Antonio Costa has proposed a smaller EU budget for 2028-2034, including a 3% tax on major digital firms to ease member states' contributions. France has already rejected the plan as inadequate.

European Council President Antonio Costa has proposed a new draft for the next EU budget.

Among other things, he is suggesting a tax on big tech firms to ease the contributions of member states, some of which strongly criticised earlier proposals.

The EU budget is theEuropean Union’s shared budget and it is used for things ranging from agricultural subsidies, infrastructure projects to border control and defence.

A first draft for the 2028 to 2034 budget was proposed by the EU Commission in 2025 but was criticised, especially by a group of northern European members including Germany who wanted the budget to be much smaller.

Following that criticism, the European Council President has now suggested a new draft, which is smaller by €204 billion.

But divisions among member states appear to remain, with France immediately coming out against Costa’s new draft, saying it was "deeply disappointing."

The proposal includes a string of new "revenue channels," explicitly intended to "to keep national contributions at a reasonable level," so that member state will have to contribute less than otherwise would be the case.

The biggest splash would likely come from a tax on companies with an annual turnover from digital activities of at least 50 million euros in the EU and 750 million euros worldwide.

Those firms would be subject to "an annual contribution corresponding to 3% of the annual net turnover from online advertising, digital intermediation services and user-generated data," according to Costa.

Aside from the tech tax, the "balanced basket of new own resources" also includes taxes on tobacco products and electronic waste.

Neither the EU Commission nor European Council President Costa will have the final word on the budget. Costa's plan will now be at the centre of hours of talks when EU leaders meet in Brussels next week.

"This is a realistic budget that addresses our new priorities while preserving our established policies," Costa wrote about his proposal. "I am confident that this proposal is a good basis for moving towards a final agreement."

But an initial reaction from France suggests that any final agreement might still be some way off.

The French presidency said the budget "falls well short ​of ‌what is needed, particularly on ‌the Common Agricultural Policy, ‌notably ​to guarantee income support for farmers."

"As it ⁠stands, ​this proposal ​from the President ​of the European ‌Council does not ​provide ⁠a basis for an ⁠agreement."

#128. When you see smoke, run: Rohingya who escaped Myanmar’s genocide tell their stories

Survivors recount rivers red with blood and burning nearby villages serving as an urgent warning to flee their homes

Smoke was often the signal to run – seen from a distance, it was the indication for Rohingya that a neighbouring village was under attack and that theirs would be next.

In other places, they saw the rivers run red and would pull out the bodies, perform quick burial rites and then flee.

What happened during the escape from Myanmar – a journey through forests and over hills and rivers while being battered by monsoon rains – was a key part of the ruling made this week in The Hague that Myanmar had carried out a genocide against the Rohingya.

The international court of justice (ICJ) said it was not enough to say they had faced ethnic cleansing when soldiers had also killed those trying to escape – Myanmar was trying to destroy the group as a people.

Stories of that violence in 2017, of organised mass killing, sexual attacks and the complete destruction of their villages, came with the refugees who arrived in neighbouring Bangladesh, eventually numbering more than 700,000 and settling in what has become the world’s largest refugee camp.

For months, they would arrive from places such as Tula Toli, where thousands were rounded up, killed and buried in mass graves. In other villages, men were captured and killed by the security forces.

Many survivors had escaped an attack on their own village, only to watch their relatives being killed in the villages where they had sought safety.

Almost a decade later, the majority of Rohingya who arrived in 2017 remain in the refugee camps, joining those who had already been there since the early 1990s, having escaped previous military operations and ever-increasing persecution.

The ICJ ruling has come at a particularly harsh time for Rohingya refugees, in a year in which hundreds have died at sea trying to escape the refugee camps, and in which countries such as Malaysia and India have spoken about returning the Rohingya to Myanmar.

Rohingya campaigners are hoping that this ruling will put an end to that.

Rohingya refugees still live in fragile shelters made of bamboo and tarpaulin sheets. They are denied the right to leave the camps, to work or to seek education.

Below are some of the stories from Rohingya who arrived in Bangladesh in 2017, having escaped Tula Toli and the surrounding area.

Sitting in the background, almost unnoticed as Momtaz Begum recollected how Myanmar forces had killed her husband and children and then raped her in front of her lone surviving child, was a woman who seemed to know every word of the story.

It was not because she had heard it before. Dildar Begum had experienced exactly the same violence as her sister had, in their village, Tula Toli.

In fact, they had for months believed each other dead until they discovered that they had both made it to Bangladesh and lived in different parts of the camp.

Each had only one surviving daughter, both of whom had similar scars along the back of their heads.

Twice previously Anwara Begum had lived as a refugee in Bangladesh. Once as a child in 1978 and then again in the early 1990s, as a young mother who lost her son to a respiratory disease during a rainy season spent in a flimsy tent.

When she was forced to return to Myanmar, though the persecution she fled there had not stopped, Anwara swore to herself that she would never become a refugee again.

She broke that oath when she saw the river by her village filled with bodies floating from Tula Toli.

Her husband first pulled from the river as many bodies as he could to bury and then they gathered some belongings and headed westwards to the River Naf – the border with Bangladesh.

To cross, she handed over all the gold she owned, equal to US $200 (£150), to a Bangladeshi boatman.

Elderly people were not spared the Myanmar military’s attacks. Like so many others, Nur Alam had to be carried on the shoulders of his children on his journey to Bangladesh.

His 10-year-old grandson, Mamoon, held his hand. Once in the camp, they stuck to each other.

Alam’s arrival in Bangladesh was his fourth time being a refugee there. He was first expelled from Myanmar – known as Burma until 1989 – during military operations in the 1950s when Bangladesh was not yet an independent country.

#123. EU and Indonesia sign trade deal

A deal covering 730 million people promises lower tariffs and new investment opportunities as the European Union seeks to diversify trade ties amid growing global economic tensions.

The European Union and Indonesia signed a free trade deal on Saturday, describing it as a new chapter in economic relations between the two sides.

"This is one of the most ambitious deals the European Union has concluded in Asia," Ursula von der Leyen, president of the European Commission, said at the signing ceremony on Saturday.

"Together, we represent a market of 730 million people," she added.

The Indonesia-European Union Comprehensive Economic Partnership Agreement (CEPA) and the additional Investment Protection Agreement (IPA) are designed to reduce trade barriers and attract investment in strategic sectors such as electric vehicles, electronics, and pharmaceuticals.

The agreement comes after almost 10 years of negotiations and is part of a wider series of trade deals by the EU.

The bloc hopes to reduce its reliance on individual economic partners as trade with both China and the US has become more difficult in recent years.

Von der Leyen also stressed the importance of international cooperation, saying that "in a time when others are closing their doors, we keep ours open."

The deal sees tariffs on about 98% of EU exports to Indonesia to be done away with. The EU estimates that European companies could save more than €600 million (around €658 million) per year.

"The benefits for our exporters are concrete and measurable," Indonesian Minister of Economic Affairs Airlangga Hartarto said, hailing a "new chapter" in economic relations with the EU. He said the deal would benefit his country’s main exports to Europe, including palm oil, footwear and textiles.

Palm oil and deforestation had been major stumbling blocks during the negotiations, and the agreement includes mechanisms for cooperation on regulatory and sustainability issues as well as a dedicated protocol on palm oil to address environmental concerns.

The EU parliament is now expected to greenlight the deal while Indonesian authorities will also complete the ratification process so it can enter into force in 2027.

Israeli tanks rolled along the Salah al-Din road, Gaza’s main north-south route, a day after the US-backed Board of Peace, a body overseeing the ceasefire, accused Israel and Hamas of failing to honour their commitments to end hostilities.

An Israeli military spokesperson said troops were carrying out an operation targeting “Hamas infrastructure” west of the yellow line in central Gaza.

The operation lasted about 24 hours and on Thursday the military said it had been “completed”.

The report, seen by Reuters, warned that “a ceasefire that delivers nothing visible cannot be sustained indefinitely”.

Gaza’s health authorities say at least 1,600 Palestinians have been killed since the ceasefire came into effect. The Israeli military says five of its soldiers have been killed over the same period.

In June, an ⁠independent UN inquiry determined that Israel was continuing to commit genocide by deliberately targeting Palestinian children. #118. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.
