#50. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Russia’s independent news outlet Fontanka is reporting that the cafe belongs to Prigozhin, on the basis of the Concord company’s ownership mentioned on the cafe’s receipt.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

CNN has not been able to independently verify Prigozhin’s claims and whether he is the legal owner of the cafe.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#48. Deal with EU not dependent on tuition fees agreement, Miliband says

Watch: Miliband spoke to the BBC following the summit in Brussels

A deal agreed between the UK and the European Union on Friday will come into force regardless of whether the two sides can negotiate a separate tuition fees agreement, Foreign Secretary Ed Miliband has told the BBC.

The measures agreed in Brussels include scrapping most checks on British food exports in return for the UK following some EU regulations from 2028, and a new youth mobility scheme.

However, a proposal from the bloc to lower the level of tuition fees paid by EU students in the UK has not been agreed.

European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said she was "confident" a solution would be found, with Prime Minister Andy Burnham adding "me too" during a press conference announcing the deal.

Meanwhile, asked in his interview with the BBC's Sunday with Laura Kuenssberg programme whether rejoining the EU would be looked at, Miliband said "all the options will be on the table".

It came after the prime minister said that he would discuss possible ideas for even closer ties with the bloc in the coming months.

What is the EU-UK youth experience scheme and when will it start?

Burnham gets his first taste of EU dealmaking and seems hungry for more

Faisal Islam: This cost-cutting UK-EU deal doesn't mean lower prices will follow

UK and EU leaders have hailed the closer cooperation agreement, with Burnham calling the deal a "win-win" that could lower the cost of living and reduce red tape.

The prime minister said the Treasury believes the deal could boost the UK economy by up to Â£17bn a year.

EU sources had earlier suggested that the bloc might hold up applying the wider deal until it was agreed that EU students who wanted to study in the UK would pay the same fees as British students.

Miliband said it was "absolutely clear" the agreements on food exports, energy and youth mobility would apply, even though there has not been a deal on tuition fees.

"President von der Leyen... was very clear about this at the press conference we just had. She said there are those three agreements and then there's a separate piece of paper and a separate issue, which is looking at the issue of tuition," he said.

Asked again whether the agreements would definitely become law, the foreign secretary said: "Yes, absolutely."

"The dates are set out in 2028 when these things are coming in, we're introducing legislation in Parliament, so definitely."

Miliband said there would be "no cost" to the UK taxpayer for anything agreed at Friday's summit, adding that there would be no new contributions to the EU budget.

He said previously announced agreements on EU schemes like Erasmus+ were a "separate decision" and the summit "was not about cost to the taxpayer".

Speaking on Friday, Burnham argued that polling suggests the UK public "want closer ties" with the EU and the deal "delivers that".

Miliband told the BBC that "of course all the options will be on the table" when pressed on whether rejoining the bloc would be considered, adding that "we will look at the benefits and the costs of all the different options".

He said that while he accepted the result of the 2016 Brexit referendum, "the world has changed massively".

"Europe is changing too, so we've got to look at what that means. It's not about this parliament, we're respecting the red lines in this parliament," he added.

Conservative leader Kemi Badenoch said the deal was "not fair", arguing that UK farmers would have to follow rules set in Brussels and young people from the EU would "compete" with young Britons struggling to get jobs at home.

She also claimed Burnham had "plucked" the Â£17bn figure out of thin air, and said the only modelling she had seen of how the agreement could boost the economy put the figure at "0.0%".

Reform UK leader Nigel Farage said Friday marked a "betrayal of the British people" and a good day for the EU to "try and humiliate" the UK.

Farage, who was a leading figure in the 2016 campaign to get Britain out of the EU, wrote on social media: "Like every prime minister before him, Burnham is a total fraud."

Liberal Democrat leader Sir Ed Davey said there needed to be more than "tweaks" to the UK's relationship to the EU.

"A youth mobility scheme is a big step forward, but it's nowhere near enough. We need a whole new deal that actually works for Britain, and fast."

Speaking to BBC Radio 4's Today programme, the University of Manchester's vice chancellor Professor Duncan Ivison said closer UK-EU relations were a "good thing" but "the issue of tuition fees is something we do have to figure out because there is a cost".

He warned that EU students being allowed to pay domestic university fees would "take Â£500m, we estimate, out of the higher education sector at a time where there's a lot of pressure on the sector".

He added: "If EU students come and are paying domestic fees, it's not clear we'd be able to increase the number of places for EU students anyway."

Environment Secretary Dame Angela Eagle said the Treasury and Department for Education would work with universities to find a solution during negotiations.

She told BBC Breakfast: "I don't think anybody wants to do deals that negatively impact anything. We want win-win deals."

#45. Nasdaq erases all 2026 gains as AI sell-off dampens Anthropic’s second day of trading

The Nasdaq lost trillions in value, with the AI startup’s IPO failing to buoy it as investors question huge sums being poured into AI buildout

Who are Anthropic’s seven co-founders, now worth $180bn?

As Anthropic’s stock dropped during its second day of trading, the wider stock market dipped with it. On Friday, the Nasdaq Composite erased all its gains for 2026 and closed down 5% from its value on 1 January. The Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 closed in correction territory.

Anthropic’s record-breaking IPO failed to stem a global sell-off in AI-linked companies, and the tech-heavy Nasdaq erased some $4tn in market value this week, according to the Wall Street Journal.

Shares in the frontier AI company behind the Claude chatbot, which floated in the US on Thursday, fell by 9% on Friday, ceding most of the gains from the biggest initial public offering in history. They dropped to $88.30, towards the $83 level at which Anthropic priced its stock market debut.

Anthropic’s first two days as a listed company have been volatile. Its shares jumped by 17% on Thursday, after briefly surging by almost 40% when trading in the firm began.

US technology stocks followed Anthropic down on Friday, with the memory chipmaker SanDisk falling 14% and rival Micron down 12%. Nvidia fell 14%, eliminating hundreds of billions of dollars in market cap.

Neil Wilson, a UK investor strategist at Saxo, said there was “just relentless selling in tech”. He warned: “Anthropic shares tumbled to take an $85 handle as the post-IPO euphoria gives way to the reality of a steep sell-off across the tech and AI space that shows no sign of easing just yet.”

Global markets slumped on Friday too; Asian stock markets recorded their worst week since 2020, as shares in big chip companies fell. In Taiwan, stocks dropped 6.7% to hit their lowest point since mid-April, with the chip maker TSMC down about 6%.

The scale of the decline in Taiwan prompted the chair of its stock exchange to pledge to ensure the stable operation ⁠of capital markets, and reassure investors they should remain confident in the fundamentals of Taiwanese companies.

In Japan, the Nikkei index fell 5%, while South Korea’s tech-heavy Kospi index lost 5.5%. Shares in the tech investor SoftBank had their worst day in more than two years, down 14.5% in Tokyo, amid worries around its aggressive bets on AI.

European stock markets posted their biggest weekly loss since March 2025, with the pan-European Stoxx 600 dropping 1.9% on Friday. London’s blue-chip stock index hit a five-month low.

The sell-off followed losses on Wall Street on Thursday, where the main indices all fell by more than 2% on a day dominated by Anthropic’s IPO.

Chris Beauchamp, the chief market analyst at the investing and trading platform IG, said Anthropic could be “draining market liquidity”, as investors sell shares in other companies to raise funds to pay for stock in the newly floated AI firm.

Get set for the working day – we'll point you to all the business news and analysis you need every morning

“The slide from yesterday’s highs sends a message that there might be more at work here than investors realise, and while brokers are scrambling to buy more, that liquidity has to come from somewhere, and the other tech names may be the first ones to feel the pressure,” Beauchamp said.

Anthropic’s flotation has failed to ease worries around the AI trade, as investors increasingly question whether companies can deliver the returns necessary to justify the billions of dollars being poured into the AI buildout.

Daniela Hathorn, a senior market analyst at Capital.com, said: “The IPO demonstrated that demand for AI exposure remains strong, but it has not been enough to overcome concerns about elevated valuations, profitability and the sustainability of investment spending.

“The broader market mood has shifted from buying the dip towards questioning whether the correction has further to run. The weakness in equal-weighted indices suggests that selling pressure is no longer confined to a handful of technology giants, while Europe’s recent outperformance has also faded.”

Nvidia shares fell byafter California served the chip company with a subpoena as part of an investigation into whether its advanced semiconductors ended up in China.

Britain’s FTSE 100 share index dropped by 5.1% this week, its biggest weekly fall since the start of the Iran war.

Fears that US central bankers may raise interest rates to fight inflation have lifted the dollar to a 19-month high, and pushed down the gold price – with gold on track for its worst week since March.

Bitcoin hit its lowest level in four months, dropping below the $60,000 point for the first time since early July.

#43. Man convicted of starting Palisades fire in Los Angeles

Homes along Los Angeles' Sunset Boulevard could be seen engulfed in flames during the fires of January 2025

A jury in Los Angeles has found a 30-year-old man guilty of starting one of the most destructive wildfires in California's history.

Jonathan Rinderknecht was convicted of arson after starting the fires that swept through Pacific Palisades and parts of Malibu in January last year, killing 12 people and destroying more than 6,800 buildings in the US state.

The conviction was handed down nearly five months after deadlocked jurors led to the judge declaring a mistrial.

Rinderknecht, who had pleaded not guilty, was convicted of two federal charges. He is due to be sentenced on 17 March when he faces between five and 20 years in prison on each charge.

Jonathan Rinderknecht's arrest in October last year

Rinderknecht was found guilty of lighting a fire on 1 January last year which burned undetected deep in root systems before flaring back up six days later.

One of the charges was for starting the original brush fire, while the other centred on the damage caused when it reignited into a catastrophic blaze.

"We were determined to hold him accountable, and today's guilty verdict delivers a measure of justice for the families who lost loved ones and the thousands whose lives were upended," said Bill Essayli, who leads the US Attorney's Office for the Central District of California.

Rinderknecht is a Los Angeles resident and former ride-share delivery driver.

Defence attorney Steve Haney had argued at trial that prosecutors lacked sufficient physical evidence or records to prove their case.

Lawyers for the defendant said they were "disappointed" by the verdict but respected the jury's decision.

Los Angeles Mayor Karen Bass, who was widely criticised for her handling of the fires, said in a statement that "no verdict can bring back the lives lost or erase the pain families have endured".

"So many are still struggling to rebuild," she said. "For those still displaced, the devastation remains a daily reality."

Prosecutors had argued that Rinderknecht was driven by revenge, anger and loneliness because he had nowhere to go for the holiday.

They cited geolocation data from his iPhone, security camera footage that showed his car's location and witness testimony from passengers.

They alleged that Rinderknecht had a "deeply entrenched belief that the wealthy were destroying the world" and in turn purposely started the blaze in the affluent neighbourhood.

The fire was one of several that ignited back-to-back across the LA region in January, which in total left 31 people dead and destroyed 16,000 structures.

#42. Trump threatens ‘tremendous penalties’ for US tech firms that build datacenters abroad

President gives wide-ranging White House press conference and says he has ‘fantastic relationships’ with Putin and Xi

Donald Trump threatened on Friday to impose “tremendous penalties” on American tech companies that build datacenters outside the US, though he gave no details and told reporters he had not yet informed his own officials about the plan.

In a discursive press conference from the White House briefing room, Trump also said he would “look into” the report that Iran had been planning to possibly detonate a “dirty bomb” in Washington DC, and backed JD Vance over comments the vice-president made blaming Republican midterm losses on the Iran war.

Trump also expounded on his friendship with the Russian president Vladimir Putin, whom he met alongside China’s Xi Jinping at the Apec summit on Thursday, adding he had “fantastic relationships” with both autocratic leaders.

At the White House, Trump told reporters he wanted datacenters built by US companies to stay on US soil.

“We’re not allowing datacenters to go into other countries, because China will take all of them, and we’re not allowing that to happen. And we’re going to put tremendous penalties on companies that try to do it,” he said.

He said Americans should welcome datacenters, and urged communities to extract concessions from developers, repeating a point he made before the midterms.

“You ought to take advantage of the datacenters. Make them pay you, make them pay your community, but don’t shut them off,” he said.

With many Americans turning against the datacenters being built near their homes, the US tech industry is making a show of conciliation by promising to take fewer government handouts, to be more transparent, and to give more money to towns and cities willing to allow server warehouses to be built.

The Trump administration on Monday issued new rules to restrict remote access to advanced US computing chips and servers, in a move it said was designed to limit China’s ability to use US chips to train its artificial intelligence models – though a House committee said on Wednesday the US had signed off on sales of nearly 900,000 AI chips to China in six months.

China’s ability to obtain advanced AI chips made by the US firm Nvidia, in which Trump is an investor, has been spotlighted in recent weeks after its CEO, Jensen Huang, was fired over his affair with a woman alleged to be spying for China.

In the same press conference, Trump was asked about reports Iran was planning to potentially detonate an explosive laced with nuclear material – a so-called “dirty bomb” – in Washington DC. The president called it a “rumour” and said: “If they do, we’re going to fight.”

He added: “We know a lot about what’s happening in Iran. We know everything they say. We know who they are, where they are. We’ll find out about it.”

Responding to a question about reports of a rift with Vance, Trump said the vice-president’s remark blaming the Republican collapse on the Iran war was “a very accurate statement”.

“I didn’t say I was unhappy with it,” the president said. “I would have added one other thing if it were asked … would you do it again? And absolutely, the answer is yes, because we cannot allow Iran to have a nuclear weapon.”

Trump was asked whether Vladimir Putin could really be considered a friend to America since Russia is a close ally of Iran.

“I can’t speak to America,” he said, adding: “I consider [that] I have fantastic relationships with Putin. That’s a very good thing. I have fantastic relationships with President Xi. That’s a good thing, and our country is safe because of that.”

He praised Putin as he repeated his grievances over the Department of Justice’s investigation into the Russian attempts to influence US voters ahead of the 2016 presidential election.

“I’ve been dealing with Putin … [since] the fake Russia, Russia, Russia hoax,” he said.

Trump also spoke about two prominent Democrats. He said Gina Hinojosa, who won the race for Texas governor over Trump’s Republican ally Greg Abbott, called him. “She was really nice,” Trump said. “It was a very productive call. And she wants to have a strong border, and I think we’ll be able to work something out.”

And he said Hakeem Jeffries – who Trump previously called a “thug” but is now in line to be the next speaker of the House, and the first Black speaker – is “a smart guy. He’s a streetwise guy.”

#38. Denmark accuses Russia of plotting to assassinate US-Ukrainian rapper

Denmark has accused Russian intelligence services of plotting to assassinate a Ukrainian-American near Copenhagen, saying they had "planned" and "initiated" the attempt.

Denmark's security and intelligence agency (PET) said it had stopped individuals who were intending to kill the person, named by Danish media as rapper Kyivstoner, at a concert in September. Two people were charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service, and pleaded not guilty.

Denmark's foreign minister said Russia's defence attachÃ© will now be expelled.

Russia's embassy in Denmark called the allegations "unsubstantiated" and said Copenhagen failed to provide evidence of Moscow's involvement.

In a statement, the embassy claimed a "Danish model" had emerged, which it described as a "Russian trace" allegedly being discovered and "exploited for a propaganda spectacle". It accused Denmark of "provocations" intending to "frighten the Danish population in order to justify... a prolonged confrontation with Russia".

Russian state media cite the foreign ministry in Moscow as saying it will respond to the expulsion of its official "accordingly".

In a statement on Saturday, European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen said: "I strongly condemn the assassination attempt against a US-Ukrainian citizen in Denmark, orchestrated by Russian intelligence services.

"This was an attack on EU soil. And it is unacceptable," she said, adding that "Russia's hybrid threats are becoming less and less hybrid, and more and more real."

The rapper's real name is Albert Vasiliev.

PET said the individuals who allegedly tried to assassinate him were associated with a banned Danish gang named Loyal to Familia. The group is "known for extensive, serious and violent criminal activity", according to the agency.

PET, which worked with police on the case, said three individuals were stopped while travelling by car near the music venue where Kyivstoner was set to perform on 25 September.

Firearms and knives were found in the vehicle, PET said.

Since then, two people linked to the case have been charged with attempted murder and assisting a foreign intelligence service.

Both individuals pleaded not guilty to the charges, local media reported.

In July, Russia's FSB security service accused Vasiliev and the Ukrainian authorities of organising a "terrorist attack" on military facilities and personnel in Russia. The rapper denied this allegation on social media.

In a statement on Friday, Danish Foreign Minister Lars LÃ¸kke Rasmussen said the alleged assassination attempt was Russian "intimidation" and an attempt to make Denmark "waver" in its support for Ukraine.

Russia's defence attachÃ© must leave the country within seven days, Rasmussen said.

He also said Denmark wants the EU to restrict the ability of Russians to travel throughout Europe, saying even though Copenhagen was already very reluctant to issue visas, Russians could still get a Schengen visa in another country and travel to Denmark.

Copenhagen wants these visas to apply only in the country that has issued them, he added.

#35. China commits to low-carbon electrification and help for others to improve

Exclusive: Countries at Cop31 are being urged to commit to deriving 35% of their energy from electricity by 2035

China is pushing for rapid electrification of its economy using low-carbon energy and will help other countries do the same, a senior member of the Chinese delegation at the Cop31 UN climate summit has said.

Wang Yi, a professor of energy and environment policy at the Chinese Academy of Sciences and a senior adviser to China’s government, said: “End-use electrification is a very clear direction. We can follow this direction while making the source of electricity clean. That’s the way to go forward.”

China is the biggest emitter of greenhouse gases by far, producing more than a quarter of the world’s total, so its push for electrification is significant.

At Cop31, countries are being asked to sign up to a target of deriving 35% of final energy consumption from electricity by 2035. Australia, the president of the negotiations, and the host nation Turkey have pushed the target and on Friday night Australia and China signed a joint statement endorsing the “35:35” goal.

But in the second draft text of the probable outcome from Cop31, released on Thursday, the electrification pledge was downgraded to one of a suite of voluntary options countries can take up to reduce their greenhouse gas emissions.

In an interview with the Guardian at the talks, Wang said: “China will do our best to realise clean end-use electrification, and also help other countries to improve. China has significantly reduced the cost of clean technology and products and we would like to share our experience with other countries.”

Experts have said electrification is the next vital step in the shift to a low-carbon economy, as the only way to diminish the close to 80% of fossil fuels still used for primary energy, mostly for transport, heating and industry.

While civil society organisations want any commitment made on electrification to specify that it must be met from clean sources, a pledge that leaves the choice to countries is likely to have more widespread support, as many – including China and India – are resistant to anything they feel smacks of “top down” interference in national plans.

But campaigners argue that allowing individual countries to decide risks leaving the door open to more gas-fired power plants. Some compromise language is also possible that would urge countries to decarbonise their power.

Last year, electricity made up nearly 30% of China’s energy use and it is on track to make that 35% by 2030. Electricity is several times more efficient than fossil fuels, meaning that it takes far less energy to run electric vehicles, heat pumps and industrial processes than their fossil fuel counterparts.

Wang said China was happy with the 2035 target, but that it would be harder to meet if the electricity was required to come entirely from low-carbon sources. “For this target, no problem. But if you think about ensuring clean electrification, that may be a little bit difficult, even for China. For other countries, it’s even more difficult,” he said.

China still has a large number of coal-fired power stations and is planning some new ones, though fewer than before. Wang said this would be used for back-up during the transition to clean energy.

One of the key issues at Cop31 is the “transition away from fossil fuels”, which was one of the resolutions passed at the Cop28 summit in Dubai in 2023, but which countries have failed to flesh out since. The phrase was included in the first draft of the “cover decision” outcome from Cop31, presented on Monday, but excised from the second draftissued on Thursday, to the dismay of many countries and campaigners.

Wang said: “We agree with paragraph 28 of the UAE consensus [the transition resolution]. But maybe different countries have different roadmaps. It’s very difficult to have only one unified roadmap for every country. So maybe we will help other countries to have their own country-based solutions, to realise their own national way of establishing a new renewable-based power system.”

China is also concerned about trade barriers to its clean technology exports, including the tariffs that the US under Donald Trump has placed on Chinese goods, and the “carbon border adjustment mechanism” (CBAM) imposed by the EU. The CBAM penalises imports from countries with looser regulations on carbon than the EU, which forces companies in high-carbon sectors to pay for their carbon emissions in order to level the playing field.

Some smaller developing countries have criticised the EU for its CBAM, warning that it will hamper their manufacturing industries. The EU said it had processes in place to ensure smaller developing countries were not hurt.

Wang called for more work on trade issues at Cops. “They must come together to find a unified approach,” he said. “China and other developing countries, including the global south, disagree [with the EU] on this. But that’s why we should continue to strengthen our dialogue.”

Jiangwen Guo, a senior research fellow at the Chatham House thinktank, said other countries could learn from China. “China has very consistent top-down policies [and] sets a clear direction with high consistency, then follows with learning by doing,” she said. “In the great energy transition, there is no one ahead of China. China is creating the model for the globe.”

Negotiators at Cop31, which was scheduled to end at 6pm on Friday, were sent home late on Friday after the talks stalled, with a new draft text of a “cover decision” marking the main outcome of the negotiations not thought likely to emerge until some time on Saturday morning. The talks are likely to continue into Saturday and potentially beyond.
